    closure contains all 32 grid nodes ($n = 32 > 25$), so all 25 axiom factors
    are skipped by the fast-path threshold.  This validates the skip logic but
    does not exercise the Steps~2--4 axiom-factor code.

    \begin{center}
    \begin{tabular}{lrrrrrr}
    \toprule
    \textbf{Config} & \textbf{$h_{\mathrm{init}}$} & \textbf{States}
      & \textbf{Plan cost} & \textbf{Axiom factors} & \textbf{Max abs.} & \textbf{Build time} \\
    \midrule
    \texttt{blind}            & -- & 302 & 4   & --                     & --     & 0.017\,s \\
    \texttt{SCCS}             & 2  & 33  & \textbf{4\,\checkmark} & 0 built, 25 skipped & 49\,616 & 15.8\,s \\
    \texttt{stateless DFP}    & 2  & 33  & \textbf{4\,\checkmark} & 0 built, 25 skipped & 49\,616 & 15.8\,s \\
    \texttt{stateless MIASM}  & 2  & 33  & \textbf{4\,\checkmark} & 0 built, 25 skipped & 12\,168 & 25.0\,s \\
    \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \end{center}

    All three merge strategies return plan cost~4, matching blind.
    SCCS and stateless DFP produce identical results and the same final
    abstraction (same 42 primary variables, no axiom factors). Stateless MIASM
    reaches the same heuristic quality with a substantially smaller maximum
    intermediate abstraction (12\,168 vs.\ 49\,616) and 4$\times$ less peak
    memory (23.7\,MB vs.\ 90.8\,MB), at the cost of a slower selector
    computation (25\,s vs.\ 15.8\,s).

    \textit{Coverage note:} because $n = 32$ for every derived variable in
    this instance, no axiom factors are built under any strategy.
    The Steps~2--4 code paths are not exercised here; full coverage requires
    a domain with smaller primary-variable closures.

    \paragraph{Results: \texttt{miconic-axioms} p01.}

    This is a minimal elevator instance (1 passenger, 2 floors) with 5 total
    variables, 2 of which are derived.  Each derived variable's primary-variable
    closure contains exactly $n = 1$ primary variable, making the BFS product
    space trivial.  Both axiom factors are built under all three strategies,
    confirming that the Steps~2--4 code path executes and produces correct
    results on a real domain (the first such confirmation).

    \begin{center}
    \begin{tabular}{lrrrr}
    \toprule
    \textbf{Config} & \textbf{$h_{\mathrm{init}}$} & \textbf{States}
      & \textbf{Plan cost} & \textbf{Axiom factors} \\
    \midrule
    \texttt{blind}           & -- & 3 & 2   & -- \\
    \texttt{SCCS}            & $2 = h^*$ & 3 & \textbf{2\,\checkmark} & 2 built ($n=1$ each) \\
    \texttt{stateless DFP}   & $2 = h^*$ & 3 & \textbf{2\,\checkmark} & 2 built ($n=1$ each) \\
    \texttt{stateless MIASM} & $2 = h^*$ & 3 & \textbf{2\,\checkmark} & 2 built ($n=1$ each) \\
    \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \end{center}

    All three strategies reach the perfect heuristic ($h_{\mathrm{init}} =
    2 = h^*$) and expand exactly 3 states (same as blind), since the
    abstraction is exact on this tiny instance.  Build times are under
    1.5\,ms in all cases.  The merge sequences differ between strategies
    but the final abstraction is identical.  The instance is too small to
    differentiate strategy quality; the key result is that the Phase~2
    pipeline runs without errors and the plan cost matches the blind
    reference.

    \paragraph{Results: \texttt{blocker} bm2 (with Improvement~1).}

    The \texttt{blocker} domain (74 variables, 8 derived) models a
    sliding-block puzzle where the goal predicate \texttt{cat-moves} is
    derived.  The instance bm2 has 3 primary nodes.  Of the 8 derived
    variables, only variable~73 is goal-derived; variables 29, 32, 33,
    35, 38, 39, and 40 appear only in operator preconditions and do not
    receive axiom factors under the current implementation.

    Improvement~1 adds an all-goal-state check immediately after the BFS
    in \texttt{build\_axiom\_factor}: if every reachable abstract state
    satisfies the derived goal conditions, the factor contributes
    $h = 0$ everywhere and is skipped before being inserted into the
    factored transition system.  For variable~73, the BFS explores
    $n = 6$ primary variables with 27 relevant operators and discovers
    160 reachable states, \emph{all of which are goal states}.  The check
    fires and the factor is discarded with the log message:
    \begin{quote}\small
    \texttt{Axiom factor: all 160 reachable state(s) are goal states
    (h=0 everywhere); skipping factor (heuristic remains admissible).}
    \end{quote}

    \begin{center}
    \begin{tabular}{lrrrr}
    \toprule
    \textbf{Config} & \textbf{$h_{\mathrm{init}}$} & \textbf{States}
      & \textbf{Plan cost} & \textbf{Axiom factor (var 73)} \\
    \midrule
    \texttt{blind}           & -- & 7  & 3   & -- \\
    \texttt{SCCS}            & 0  & 11 & \textbf{3\,\checkmark} & all-goal $\to$ skipped \\
    \texttt{stateless DFP}   & 0  & 11 & \textbf{3\,\checkmark} & all-goal $\to$ skipped \\
    \texttt{stateless MIASM} & 0  & 11 & \textbf{3\,\checkmark} & all-goal $\to$ skipped \\
    \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \end{center}

    All three strategies report \texttt{Number of factors kept: 0}.  The
    primary merged factor (max intermediate abstraction: 10 states) is
    itself trivially all-goal once the entire variable set is merged and
    bisimulation-shrunk, so $h = 0$ everywhere regardless.  Admissibility
    is preserved: plan cost~3 is optimal.  The search expands 11 states
    vs.\ 7 for blind—slightly worse, as expected when no heuristic
    guidance is available.

    \textbf{Effect of Improvement~1 here.}
    The all-goal check fires correctly and saves the cost of inserting and
    merging a useless axiom factor.  However, the expansion count is
    unchanged: the primary merged factor is independently trivially
    all-goal, so $h = 0$ with or without the axiom factor.  \texttt{blocker}
    demonstrates that the guard is correct but that the gain depends on
    whether the primary factor is also non-trivial.  Domains where the
    primary factor \emph{is} informative while the axiom factor is trivial
    would show a concrete improvement.

    \paragraph{Results: \texttt{blocks-axioms} probBLOCKS-4-0.}

    The \texttt{blocks-axioms} domain expresses \texttt{clear} and
    \texttt{handempty} as derived predicates that are used in operator
    preconditions.  The instance has 34 variables (5 derived) and a plan
    cost of 6 (stack $b$--$a$--$c$--$d$).

    All three strategies build 5 axiom factors for derived variables 0, 26,
    28, 29, and 30.  Each factor's BFS closure contains $n = 4$ primary
    variables and 40 relevant operators.  Unlike \texttt{blocker}, the final
    merged factor is \emph{non-trivial}: bisimulation preserves the
    goal-distance structure, yielding a perfect heuristic.

    \begin{center}
    \begin{tabular}{lrrrr}
    \toprule
    \textbf{Config} & \textbf{$h_{\mathrm{init}}$} & \textbf{States}
      & \textbf{Plan cost} & \textbf{Max intermediate} \\
    \midrule
    \texttt{blind}           & -- & 85  & 6   & -- \\
    \texttt{SCCS}            & $6 = h^*$ & 7  & \textbf{6\,\checkmark} & 192 \\
    \texttt{stateless DFP}   & $6 = h^*$ & 7  & \textbf{6\,\checkmark} & 192 \\
    \texttt{stateless MIASM} & $6 = h^*$ & 7  & \textbf{6\,\checkmark} & 48  \\
    \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \end{center}

    All three strategies achieve the perfect heuristic ($h_{\mathrm{init}} =
    6 = h^*$) and expand only 7 states vs.\ 85 for blind ($12\times$
    improvement).  SCCS and DFP produce identical merge sequences and the
    same maximum intermediate abstraction of 192 states.  MIASM reaches the
    same quality with a 4$\times$ smaller intermediate abstraction (48 states)
    at identical peak memory ($\approx$14.2\,MB).  This is the strongest
    heuristic result so far in the Step~5 campaign: the axiom factors for
    \texttt{clear}/\texttt{handempty} carry tight goal-distance information
    that survives bisimulation and makes the heuristic exact on this instance.

    \paragraph{Results: \texttt{sokoban-axioms} p01.}

    The \texttt{sokoban-axioms} domain (120 variables, 40 derived) derives
    \texttt{can-reach} and \texttt{clear} predicates.  M\&S builds all
    \textbf{40 axiom factors}, ranging from $n = 1$ to $n = 3$ primary
    variables with up to 702 relevant operators per factor ($\approx$33\,s
    construction time dominated by the large $n = 3$ BFS runs).

    \begin{center}
    \begin{tabular}{lrrrrrr}
    \toprule
    \textbf{Config} & \textbf{$h_{\mathrm{init}}$} & \textbf{States}
      & \textbf{Dead ends} & \textbf{Plan cost}
      & \textbf{Max abs.} & \textbf{Build time} \\
    \midrule
    \texttt{blind}           & -- & 140 & -- & 11   & -- & 0.005\,s \\
    \texttt{SCCS}            & $\mathbf{10}$ & \textbf{14} & 20
      & \textbf{11\,\checkmark} & 2\,160 & 39.4\,s \\
    \texttt{stateless DFP}   & $\mathbf{10}$ & \textbf{14} & 20
      & \textbf{11\,\checkmark} & 5\,064 & 35.0\,s \\
    \texttt{stateless MIASM} & $\mathbf{10}$ & \textbf{14} & 20
      & \textbf{11\,\checkmark} & 2\,223 & 43.1\,s \\
    \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \end{center}

    All three strategies achieve $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 10$ (against
    $h^* = 11$, near-perfect) and expand only 14 states vs.\ 140 for
    blind ($10\times$ improvement), identifying 20 dead ends.  Plan cost~11
    matches blind on all strategies, confirming admissibility.  One factor
    is kept (non-trivial), confirming that bisimulation preserves
    goal-distance information on this domain.

    Merge-order comparison: DFP is the fastest ($35.0$\,s) but produces
    the largest maximum intermediate abstraction ($5\,064$ states).
    SCCS and MIASM both keep the intermediate below $2\,250$ states.
    MIASM is the slowest ($43.1$\,s) due to the per-pair score computation,
    but matches SCCS in abstraction quality.  All three peak at $\approx
    26.4$\,MB, confirming negligible memory overhead from the axiom factors.

    \textbf{MIASM cache bug (discovered and fixed).}
    The initial MIASM run crashed with a segmentation fault after
    71 merges (last completed: indices $(106, 240)$).  Root cause:
    \texttt{MergeScoringFunctionMIASM::initialize} sized its
    score-cache 2D vector as $2 n - 1$ where $n = 120$ (task variables),
    yielding a $239 \times 239$ cache.  With 40 axiom factors appended,
    the initial FTS holds $n + k = 160$ factors and the merge loop
    produces indices up to $2 \times 160 - 2 = 318$, causing an
    out-of-bounds write.  Fix: count derived variables in
    \texttt{initialize} and size the cache as
    $2(n + n_{\mathrm{derived}}) - 1$.  Tasks without derived variables
    are unaffected.

    \paragraph{Results: \texttt{ghosh-etal-JAR-acc-cc2} \texttt{acc.goodgoal6.cc2.pddl}.}

    The \texttt{ghosh-etal-JAR-acc-cc2} domain (70 variables, 34 derived)
    models access-control policies; derived variables encode
    \texttt{disabled-control-*} predicates.  M\&S builds all \textbf{34
    axiom factors}, with $n = 2$ to $n = 10$ primary variables each (BFS
    completes quickly, $\approx 0.48$\,s total).  The 66 primary variables
    are dominated by one single large SCC, so SCCS merges them in
    topological order.

    \begin{center}
    \begin{tabular}{lrrrrrr}
    \toprule
    \textbf{Config} & \textbf{$h_{\mathrm{init}}$} & \textbf{States}
      & \textbf{Dead ends} & \textbf{Plan cost}
      & \textbf{Max abs.} & \textbf{Time / Memory} \\
    \midrule
    \texttt{blind}           & -- & 199 & -- & 5   & -- & -- \\
    \texttt{SCCS}            & 2  & 49 & 0
      & \textbf{5\,\checkmark} & 49\,932 & 16.84\,s / 223\,MB \\
    \texttt{stateless DFP}   & 2  & 49 & 0
      & \textbf{5\,\checkmark} & 49\,932 & 16.91\,s / 221\,MB \\
    \texttt{stateless MIASM} & 2  & \textbf{46} & 0
      & \textbf{5\,\checkmark} & 49\,928 & 593\,s / \textbf{173\,MB} \\
    \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \end{center}

    All three strategies achieve $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 2$ (against $h^* =
    5$; limited precision) and expand far fewer states than blind (46--49
    vs.\ 199, $\approx 4\times$ improvement).  No dead ends are
    identified.  Plan cost~5 matches blind on all strategies, confirming
    admissibility.  One factor is kept (non-trivial) in all cases.

    SCCS and DFP produce the \emph{identical} maximum intermediate
    abstraction (49\,932 states) and near-identical runtimes ($\approx
    17$\,s), suggesting the merge order has minimal impact on those two
    strategies for this instance.  MIASM chooses a slightly different
    merge sequence (max abs.\ 49\,928) that expands 3 fewer states, and
    its merge order leads to $\approx 48$\,MB lower peak memory (173\,MB
    vs.\ $\approx 222$\,MB), likely because MIASM prioritises pairs with
    many dead states, causing heavier pruning of intermediate abstractions.
    However, MIASM's per-pair score computation is very expensive here:
    runtime grows to $\approx 593$\,s (35$\times$ slower than SCCS/DFP),
    reflecting the cost of evaluating all $O(n^2)$ candidate pairs in a
    70-variable task with 34 axiom factors.

    \paragraph{Results: \texttt{ghosh-etal-JAR-grid-cc2}.}

    The \texttt{ghosh-etal-JAR-grid-cc2} domain is significantly larger:
    \textbf{94\,772 variables} (vs.\ 70 for acc-cc2), with 29 derived
    variables encoding \texttt{disabled-control-*} predicates.  Blind
    A* finds the optimal plan of cost~35 by expanding 850 states (peak
    memory 138\,MB, total time 2.73\,s).

    M\&S is \textbf{not tractable} for this domain at this scale.  A
    SCCS run was attempted and aborted after observing:
    \begin{itemize}
      \item 23.6\,s and 474\,MB just to build 94\,772 atomic transition
            systems;
      \item 110\,s for the BFS of the first axiom factor alone
            (derived variable 87\,810, 0 primary vars, 105 relevant ops)
            — slow because iterating over operators in a 94\,772-variable
            task is inherently expensive even for trivial factors;
      \item with 29 derived variables at $\sim$110\,s each, initialization
            alone would exceed 50 minutes, and the subsequent merge loop
            over $\sim$94\,800 factors would be intractable.
    \end{itemize}
    This domain is \textbf{skipped} for M\&S validation.

    \textbf{Is this a limitation of our implementation or of M\&S in general?}
    This is a \emph{fundamental limitation of the M\&S framework}, not a
    defect in our implementation.  Standard M\&S was designed for tasks
    with at most a few hundred to $\sim$1\,000 variables; 94\,772 is
    roughly 100$\times$ outside that range.  The root causes are inherent
    to the algorithm:
    \begin{itemize}
      \item M\&S builds one atomic transition system per variable, so
            memory grows linearly with the variable count from the start;
      \item the merge loop runs $n - 1$ iterations and does at least
            $O(n)$ work per iteration, giving $O(n^2)$ total — for
            $n \approx 94\,800$ that is simply not feasible;
      \item label reduction re-processes all live transition systems
            every iteration, compounding the cost.
    \end{itemize}
    Our implementation is correct: axiom handling works on all
    normally-sized domains validated in this campaign (tens to $\sim$1\,000
    variables), which is the intended operating range of M\&S.

    \textbf{Is this worth fixing?}  No, not within the scope of this
    project.  The ms-axioms work targets correctness of axiom handling
    inside M\&S, not scaling M\&S to extreme task sizes.  Furthermore,
    the 94\,772-variable instance is an outlier even within the
    \texttt{ghosh-etal-JAR} family: the acc-cc2 instance of the same
    family has only 70 variables and runs in under 17 seconds.  Handling
    $10^5$-variable tasks would require a fundamentally different
    approach (partial or local M\&S, portfolio methods, or admissible
    landmark heuristics) rather than any fix to this codebase.
\end{itemize}

    \paragraph{Results: \texttt{queens-horndl}.}

    The \texttt{queens-horndl} domain has 3\,358 variables (716 derived,
    each representing a \texttt{datalog} query predicate).  Blind A*
    solves the instance trivially: the initial state is already a goal
    state ($h^* = 0$, plan cost = 0, 0 states expanded).

    \textbf{Axiom factor construction} completes successfully ($\sim$70\,s,
    $\sim$140\,MB).  Each derived variable's primary-variable closure
    has $n = 1$, so 715 axiom factors are built without triggering the
    fast-path skip threshold.  One variable (the 716th derived variable,
    index~3357, with $n = 25$ primary variables) is skipped by the
    BFS safety net ($>100\,k$ states), confirming that boundary works
    correctly.

    \textbf{The merge loop is intractable.}  SCC decomposition
    produces a single massive SCC containing hundreds of axiom factors
    together with many primary variables.  Each merge step was measured
    at approximately 100\,s, and the full loop requires $n - 1 = 3\,357$
    iterations — an estimated \textbf{110\,hours} for a single strategy.

    \textbf{Root cause: label reduction, not bisimulation.}  The 100\,s
    per step is dominated by \texttt{exact} label reduction, which
    re-processes \emph{every live transition system} at every iteration.
    After axiom factor construction, there are approximately $3\,358 +
    715 = 4\,073$ initial factors.  With thousands of live TSes and
    thousands of iterations remaining, the cumulative label-reduction
    cost is $O(n^2)$ in the factor count — the same fundamental scaling
    problem that makes \texttt{ghosh-etal-JAR-grid-cc2} impossible, just
    at a scale where construction succeeds but the merge loop does not.

    \textbf{Does our implementation make it worse?}  Slightly.  Adding
    715 axiom factors on top of the 3\,358 atomic factors increases the
    initial pool by $\sim$21\%, which compounds the per-step label
    reduction cost.  But even without axiom factors, queens-horndl at
    3\,358 primary variables alone would be very slow for M\&S.  The
    axiom factors are not the root cause.

    \textbf{Comparison with \texttt{ghosh-etal-JAR-grid-cc2}.}  Both
    domains hit the same fundamental $O(n^2)$ scaling wall, but at
    different stages:
    \begin{itemize}
      \item \texttt{ghosh-etal-JAR-grid-cc2} (94\,772 vars) fails at
            \emph{atomic construction}: iterating over operators in a
            94\,772-variable task is already slow, and 94\,000 merge
            iterations would be obviously impossible.
      \item \texttt{queens-horndl} (3\,358 vars) passes construction
            but fails in the \emph{merge loop}: the task size is
            borderline, construction works, but $\sim$4\,000 merge
            steps with thousands of live TSes for label reduction is
            prohibitive.
    \end{itemize}
    Both cases are fundamental M\&S limits, not implementation defects.

    \textbf{Is this worth fixing?}  No, for the same reasons as
    \texttt{ghosh-etal-JAR-grid-cc2}.  Fixing the quadratic scaling of
    label reduction would be a core M\&S research problem entirely
    separate from axiom handling.  The ms-axioms project targets
    \emph{correctness} of axiom handling inside M\&S on the domains for
    which M\&S is tractable (tens to $\sim$1\,000 variables); scalability
    beyond that range is out of scope.

    Additionally, since $h^* = 0$ for this instance, any admissible
    heuristic trivially matches optimal.  Even if M\&S completed, the
    result would provide no meaningful validation of heuristic quality.

    This domain is \textbf{skipped} for M\&S validation.

    \paragraph{Results: \texttt{social-planning} p01.}

    The \texttt{social-planning} domain models social manipulation (agents
    taking, dropping, and moving objects to induce beliefs).  The instance
    has 105 variables, of which 53 are derived (motive/reason predicates).
    Blind A* finds the optimal plan of cost~8 by expanding 21\,109 states.

    \textbf{Axiom factor construction} builds 49 factors successfully and
    skips 4 (variables 94--97, $n = 11$ each — BFS safety net triggers at
    100\,k states).  The most expensive factors have $n = 9$ primary
    variables (variables 88--91 and 99--102, $\sim$1.2\,s each); total
    construction time is $\sim$9.8\,s at $\sim$198\,MB peak.

    \begin{center}
    \begin{tabular}{lrrrrrr}
    \toprule
    \textbf{Config} & \textbf{$h_{\mathrm{init}}$} & \textbf{States}
      & \textbf{Plan cost} & \textbf{Factors} & \textbf{Max abs.} & \textbf{Time} \\
    \midrule
    \texttt{blind}           & -- & 21\,109 & 8   & --                  & --     & 0.39\,s \\
    \texttt{SCCS}            & 2  & 8\,905  & \textbf{8\,\checkmark} & 49 built, 4 skipped & 20\,664 & 12.5\,s \\
    \texttt{stateless DFP}   & 2  & 8\,905  & \textbf{8\,\checkmark} & 49 built, 4 skipped & 20\,664 & 12.4\,s \\
    \texttt{stateless MIASM} & 2  & 8\,905  & \textbf{8\,\checkmark} & 49 built, 4 skipped & 25\,572 & 51.1\,s \\
    \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \end{center}

    All three strategies confirm plan cost~8 $= h^*$.  SCCS and stateless
    DFP are nearly identical in merge order and produce the same maximum
    intermediate abstraction (20\,664 states).  Stateless MIASM takes
    $\sim$4$\times$ longer (51\,s vs.\ 12.5\,s) and reaches a slightly
    larger maximum intermediate abstraction (25\,572), but yields the same
    $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 2$ and identical search effort (8\,905 states
    expanded).  All three use 198\,MB peak memory.

    The low $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 2$ (vs.\ $h^* = 8$) reflects the 4
    skipped factors (variables 94--97, $n = 11$): those factors encode
    the goal-relevant derived predicates that M\&S cannot build within the
    BFS safety-net limit.  Despite the underestimate, M\&S still reduces
    search effort by $2.4\times$ compared to blind (8\,905 vs.\ 21\,109
    states), confirming that even partial axiom-factor coverage provides
    useful guidance.

    \paragraph{Results: \texttt{tpsa-horndl} p01.}

    The \texttt{tpsa-horndl} domain encodes a telecom provisioning workflow
    with Datalog-style derived predicates (\texttt{datalog\_*}).  The instance
    has 103 variables, of which 30 are derived.  Blind A* finds the optimal
    plan of cost~7 ($h^* = 7$) by expanding 456\,077 states in 17.15\,s.

    \textbf{Axiom factor construction} builds all 30 factors successfully
    (none skipped).  A notable structural case is variable~0, which has
    $n = 0$ primary variables — a derived predicate with no primary-variable
    dependency — making it the first such case encountered in testing.
    Variable~22 carries the largest factor load (1\,791 relevant operators).
    SCC decomposition finds one large SCC of 22 variables
    $\{1, 22, 3, 12, 21, 2, 4, 13, 5, 14, 6, 15, 7, 16, 8, 17, 9, 18, 10, 19, 11, 20\}$,
    triggering $\sim$21 sequential bisimulation steps inside that component.

    \begin{center}
    \begin{tabular}{lrrrrrr}
    \toprule
    \textbf{Config} & \textbf{$h_{\mathrm{init}}$} & \textbf{States}
      & \textbf{Plan cost} & \textbf{Factors} & \textbf{Max abs.} & \textbf{Time} \\
    \midrule
    \texttt{blind}           & -- & 456\,077 & 7   & --          & --     & 17.15\,s \\
    \texttt{SCCS}            & 1  & 417\,929 & \textbf{7\,\checkmark} & 30 built & 34      & 46.2\,s \\
    \texttt{stateless DFP}   & 1  & 417\,929 & \textbf{7\,\checkmark} & 30 built & 1\,028  & 48.8\,s \\
    \texttt{stateless MIASM} & 1  & 417\,929 & \textbf{7\,\checkmark} & 30 built & 6\,146  & 70.1\,s \\
    \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \end{center}

    All three strategies confirm plan cost~$7 = h^*$.  Bisimulation shrinks
    aggressively: SCCS collapses the final abstraction to just 34 states,
    DFP to 1\,028, and MIASM (which chooses merge pairs to maximise
    intermediate precision) to 6\,146.  In every case the resulting
    $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 1$ is far below $h^* = 7$, providing only marginal
    guidance: M\&S prunes dead ends and expands 417\,929 states vs.\
    blind's 456\,077 ($\sim$8\% fewer), but the heuristic evaluation
    overhead means all three configurations run slower than blind overall.

    MIASM's build time of 38\,s (vs.\ $\sim$17\,s for SCCS and DFP)
    reflects expensive merge-pair scoring: the very first scoring pass
    alone takes 13.5\,s.  MIASM peak memory increase from M\&S is only
    5.4\,MB; the dominant memory cost ($\sim$258\,MB peak for the
    full process) comes from the search phase with 4.6\,M evaluated states.

    \textbf{Is this our implementation or M\&S in general?}  M\&S in
    general, and the mechanism is different from the label-reduction
    scaling problems seen in \texttt{queens-horndl} and
    \texttt{vta-horndl}.

    The decisive diagnostic is MIASM: it explicitly chooses merge pairs to
    maximise intermediate abstraction quality, yet still reaches
    $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 1$ with 6\,146 abstract states.  If the
    abstraction were merely too small (the \texttt{max\_states} limit
    cutting precision), a larger abstract state space would give a better
    heuristic.  It does not — the information is structurally absent.

    The mechanism is \emph{goal-distance information loss through
    bisimulation}.  Exact bisimulation merges abstract states whose
    future behaviour (as seen through the operators represented in the
    abstract TS) is identical.  In tpsa-horndl the Datalog-style derived
    predicates encode a sequential telecom provisioning workflow; the
    abstract transition system after merging captures the operator
    signatures but loses the finer goal-distance distinctions.  Most
    abstract non-goal states end up with estimated distance~1 because,
    from the abstraction's perspective, only one abstract step separates
    them from the abstract goal.  This happens regardless of merge order
    (SCCS gives 34 states, MIASM gives 6\,146 — same heuristic value
    either way), which confirms it is a structural property of the domain
    under bisimulation, not an accident of merge sequencing.

    Our axiom factors are not the cause.  They correctly encode each
    derived variable's relationship to its primary-variable closure
    (Step~2--4 of the algorithm).  The coarse heuristic would appear
    with or without them; if anything, the axiom factors provide the
    only goal-relevant structure that the merged TS contains.

    \textbf{Is this worth fixing?}  Not within ms-axioms scope.  The
    root cause — bisimulation collapsing goal-distance information in
    certain domain structures — is a well-known fundamental limitation
    of bisimulation-based M\&S.  Three potential remedies exist in the
    M\&S literature, all orthogonal to axiom handling:
    \begin{itemize}
      \item \emph{Alternative shrinking strategies} (e.g.\ greedy
            bisimulation, DFP-based shrinking) that sacrifice some
            minimality to preserve more distance information.
      \item \emph{Larger abstractions} (higher \texttt{max\_states}) —
            ineffective here as shown by the MIASM result.
      \item \emph{Compositional abstractions / pattern databases} that
            keep complementary sub-abstractions instead of merging
            everything into one final TS.
    \end{itemize}
    None of these address axiom correctness, which is what ms-axioms
    targets.  This domain validates the implementation successfully;
    the poor $h_{\mathrm{init}}$ is a domain property, not a defect.

    \paragraph{Testing on \texttt{vta-horndl} p01.}

    The \texttt{vta-horndl} domain has 372 total variables, of which 38 are
    derived (variables 0--35 built as axiom factors with $n=1$, 15 relevant
    operators each; variable~175 skipped at $n=34$; variable~371 skipped at
    $n=26$).  Blind A* finds the optimal plan of cost~7 ($h^*=7$) by
    expanding only 684 states in 0.034\,s — a structurally large but
    trivially easy instance.

    SCC decomposition reveals one massive SCC of $\sim$203 variables
    (variables 0--189 and several higher-indexed ones), with the remaining
    $\sim$169 variables in singleton SCCs.  M\&S must merge all
    $\sim$203 variables inside that SCC sequentially; label reduction
    rescans all live transition systems at every step.

    Observed timing (DFP-within-SCCS variant, aborted after $\sim$550\,s):
    \begin{itemize}
      \item Step~1 (merge pair 36, 69): label reduction 35\,s.
      \item Steps~2--14: $\sim$30--32\,s each per merge step.
      \item Step~15 (pair 94, 421): label reduction 62\,s (cost growing).
      \item Step~16 in progress at abort — only $\sim$15 of $\sim$200
            required steps completed.
    \end{itemize}

    At $\sim$30--60\,s per step and growing cost, completing the SCC merge
    would require several hours.

    \textbf{Root cause: label reduction inside a giant SCC.}  The 30--60\,s
    per merge step is dominated by \texttt{exact} label reduction, which
    re-processes \emph{every live transition system} at every iteration.
    At the point where the SCC merge begins, there are $\sim$372
    live TSes (all atomic factors plus 36 axiom factors, minus the 2
    skipped).  As the SCC merge proceeds, the merged TS grows larger with
    each step — more states, more transitions — making each label reduction
    pass progressively more expensive.  This is the same $O(n^2)$
    scaling diagnosed in \texttt{queens-horndl}, not a property of our
    axiom-factor code.

    \textbf{A new failure mode compared to \texttt{queens-horndl}.}
    \texttt{queens-horndl} was intractable due to its total variable count
    (3\,358 variables $\Rightarrow$ 4\,073 initial factors $\Rightarrow$
    $\sim$16\,M label-reduction work units).  \texttt{vta-horndl} is
    different: at 372 variables it is not unusually large, and M\&S has
    handled comparable counts successfully (e.g.\ \texttt{tpsa-horndl},
    103 variables, 17\,s build).  The trigger here is the \emph{causal
    graph structure}: one SCC of $\sim$203 variables forces all those
    variables to be merged sequentially in a single group, with the full
    $\sim$372 live TSes in scope for label reduction throughout.  The
    per-step cost is therefore proportional to the total factor count, not
    just the SCC size, and the $O(n^2)$ wall is hit even though the
    absolute variable count would otherwise be manageable.

    In short: a domain with a few hundred variables can be intractable
    for M\&S if its causal graph contains a large strongly-connected
    component.  Total variable count is not the only predictor.

    \textbf{Is this our implementation or M\&S in general?}  M\&S in
    general.  The \texttt{exact} label-reduction method in vanilla Fast
    Downward rescans all live TSes at every iteration; this is the
    standard implementation, not a flaw introduced by our axiom work.
    Removing all axiom factors from the pool would reduce the initial
    count by 36 ($\sim$10\%), but the 203-variable SCC would still
    require $\sim$200 sequential merge steps each scanning $\sim$330
    live TSes — still several hours.  Our axiom factors are not the
    root cause.

    \textbf{Is this worth fixing?}  No, for the same reasons as
    \texttt{queens-horndl}.  Reducing label-reduction cost would require
    a partial or lazy reduction scheme (an active M\&S research topic
    unrelated to axiom handling), or accepting a weaker label equivalence.
    The ms-axioms project targets \emph{correctness} of axiom handling on
    domains where M\&S is already tractable; scalability beyond the
    tractability frontier is out of scope.

    This domain is \textbf{skipped} for M\&S validation.

    \paragraph{Testing on \texttt{vta-roles-horndl} p01.}

    Blind A* on \texttt{vta-roles-horndl} gives 373 variables (one more
    than \texttt{vta-horndl}'s 372), 759 fact pairs, 1\,133 states
    expanded, plan cost~7 ($h^*=7$), and the same 7-action VTA service
    composition plan.  The ``roles'' variant adds one extra derived
    predicate (5 vs.\ 4 in \texttt{vta-horndl}), slightly enlarging
    the state space but leaving the domain structure essentially the same.

    Given this structural identity, M\&S will encounter the same giant
    SCC of $\sim$203 variables and the same label-reduction bottleneck
    documented for \texttt{vta-horndl}.  No additional information would
    be gained by running and aborting again.

    This domain is \textbf{skipped} for M\&S validation (same reason
    as \texttt{vta-horndl}).

    \paragraph{Results: \texttt{cats-horndl} p01.}

    The \texttt{cats-horndl} domain is a small puzzle with 25 variables,
    of which 7 are derived (variables 9, 11, 13, 16, 19, 21, 24).
    Blind A* finds the optimal plan of cost~4 ($h^* = 4$) by expanding
    19 states in 0.002\,s.

    \textbf{Axiom factor construction} builds all 7 factors successfully
    (none skipped).  Six factors have $n = 1$ primary variable each;
    variable~24 has $n = 5$.  Construction takes $<1$\,ms.  SCC
    decomposition yields only small SCCs: \{8,\,11,\,10,\,9\},
    \{18,\,19\}, \{15,\,16\}, \{12,\,13\}, and singletons — no giant
    component.

    \begin{center}
    \begin{tabular}{lrrrrrr}
    \toprule
    \textbf{Config} & \textbf{$h_{\mathrm{init}}$} & \textbf{States}
      & \textbf{Plan cost} & \textbf{Factors} & \textbf{Max abs.} & \textbf{Time} \\
    \midrule
    \texttt{blind}           & -- & 19 & 4   & --       & --  & 0.002\,s \\
    \texttt{SCCS}            & \textbf{4} & 5 & \textbf{4\,\checkmark} & 7 built & 18 & 0.009\,s \\
    \texttt{stateless DFP}   & \textbf{4} & 5 & \textbf{4\,\checkmark} & 7 built & 18 & 0.008\,s \\
    \texttt{stateless MIASM} & \textbf{4} & 5 & \textbf{4\,\checkmark} & 7 built & 18 & 0.014\,s \\
    \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \end{center}

    All three strategies achieve $h_{\mathrm{init}} = h^* = 4$ — a
    \emph{perfect} heuristic.  Only 5 states are expanded (vs.\ 19
    blind); 4 dead ends are detected immediately.  The maximum
    intermediate abstraction is just 18 states across all strategies.
    MIASM is marginally slower (0.014\,s vs.\ 0.008\,s) due to
    scoring overhead, but all three are essentially instantaneous.

    This domain is an ideal validation case for the axiom-factor
    implementation: the derived-variable structure is moderate, all
    factors build cleanly, the SCC decomposition produces no large
    components, and bisimulation retains full goal-distance information,
    yielding an exact heuristic.

    \paragraph{Results: \texttt{collab-and-comm} p01.}

    The \texttt{collab-and-comm} domain models collaborative communication
    between agents and contains 204 variables, of which approximately 60 are
    derived (variables 36--38 in two groups of $n=9$ primary variables with 3
    relevant operators each; variables 75--77 similarly; and variables 87--203
    with $n=6$ primary variables and 9 relevant operators each).  Blind A*
    finds an optimal plan of cost~6 ($h^* = 6$) by expanding 133 states in
    0.008\,s.

    \textbf{Axiom factor construction} builds all factors successfully.  The
    two groups of $n=9$ factors are the most expensive: variable~37 takes
    3.57\,s and variable~76 takes 3.66\,s due to the large reachable
    state space of their BFS expansions; total axiom factor build time is
    approximately 7.5\,s across all runs.  SCC decomposition reveals two
    medium SCCs of 36 variables each (vars 0--35 and 39--74), a large SCC
    of $\sim$108 variables (vars 78--185), and many singletons (vars 186--203).
    The 108-variable SCC is comfortably within the range handled by SCCS
    (unlike \texttt{vta-horndl}'s 203-variable SCC).

    \begin{center}
    \begin{tabular}{lrrrrrr}
    \toprule
    \textbf{Config} & \textbf{$h_{\mathrm{init}}$} & \textbf{States}
      & \textbf{Plan cost} & \textbf{Max abs.} & \textbf{M\&S time} & \textbf{Memory} \\
    \midrule
    \texttt{blind}           & -- & 133 & 6\,\checkmark & --     & 0.008\,s  & $\sim$13\,MB \\
    \texttt{SCCS}            & \textbf{4} & 18 & 6\,\checkmark & 32{,}768 & 25.4\,s  & 340\,MB \\
    \texttt{stateless DFP}   & \textbf{4} & 18 & 6\,\checkmark & 32{,}768 & 17.8\,s  & 340\,MB \\
    \texttt{stateless MIASM} & 3          & 41 & 6\,\checkmark & 31{,}265 & 527\,s   & 340\,MB \\
    \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \end{center}

    SCCS and stateless DFP both achieve $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 4$, expanding
    only 18 states (vs.\ 133 blind), in 18--25\,s.  Stateless MIASM
    achieves only $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 3$ — \emph{weaker} than SCCS/DFP
    — while taking 527\,s (about 20--30$\times$ slower).  The explanation
    is twofold.  First, MIASM's scoring function evaluates trial merges
    across all live transition systems at every step: the 108-variable SCC
    forces a pool of $\sim$200+ live TSes at peak, making each scoring
    round expensive.  Three consecutive merge steps (involving the largest
    product TSes in the SCC) each required 54--68\,s of scoring evaluation,
    accounting for most of the total runtime.  Second, MIASM's greedy
    local scoring led to a merge sequence — beginning with pairs of
    consecutive variables (0,1), (2,3), \ldots\ rather than respecting
    causal-graph SCC structure — that discarded more goal-distance
    information than the SCC-guided or DFP-guided orderings.  This
    illustrates that MIASM's expensive per-step trial-merge scoring does
    not guarantee a better (or even equal) heuristic: on domains with
    large SCCs and complex inter-variable dependencies, structure-aware
    strategies such as SCCS can outperform MIASM on both quality and speed.

    All three strategies find a plan of cost~6~\checkmark{} and use
    approximately 340\,MB of peak memory.

    \paragraph{Results: \texttt{elevator-horndl} p01.}

    The \texttt{elevator-horndl} domain (42 variables, 13 derived) models an
    elevator scheduling problem where reachability and floor-service predicates
    are expressed as derived variables.  Blind A* finds the optimal plan of
    cost~25 ($h^* = 25$) by expanding 6{,}356 states in 0.028\,s.

    M\&S builds \textbf{13 axiom factors} for derived variables 0--5 (each
    with $n = 0$ primary vars, 2 relevant ops), variables 8, 11, 14, 17, 20,
    and 23 (each with $n = 1$ primary var, 2 relevant ops), and variable 41
    (with $n = 6$ primary vars, 6 relevant ops).  The SCC structure consists
    of 6 triplets (\texttt{[24,25,23]}, \texttt{[21,22,20]}, \texttt{[18,19,17]},
    \texttt{[15,16,14]}, \texttt{[12,13,11]}, \texttt{[9,10,8]}) and all
    remaining singletons; there is no large SCC.

    \begin{center}
    \begin{tabular}{lrrrrrr}
    \toprule
    \textbf{Config} & \textbf{$h_{\mathrm{init}}$} & \textbf{States}
      & \textbf{Plan cost} & \textbf{Max abs.} & \textbf{Build time} & \textbf{Peak mem.} \\
    \midrule
    \texttt{blind}            & -- & 6{,}356 & 25   & --  & 0.028\,s & -- \\
    \texttt{SCCS}             & 15 & 1{,}893 & \textbf{25\,\checkmark} & 576 & 0.096\,s & 13\,MB \\
    \texttt{stateless DFP}    & 15 & 1{,}893 & \textbf{25\,\checkmark} & 576 & 0.101\,s & 13\,MB \\
    \texttt{stateless MIASM}  & 15 & 1{,}893 & \textbf{25\,\checkmark} & 576 & 0.190\,s & 13\,MB \\
    \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \end{center}

    All three strategies agree on every metric: $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 15$,
    1\,893 states expanded (a $3.4\times$ improvement over blind's 6\,356),
    maximum intermediate abstraction of 576 states, and peak memory of
    $\approx 13$\,MB.  Plan cost~25 matches $h^*$~\checkmark{} on all
    strategies.  MIASM is approximately $2\times$ slower at build time
    (0.190\,s vs.\ 0.10\,s for SCCS/DFP) due to per-pair scoring over the
    full set of live transition systems, but the extra cost yields no quality
    difference on this instance.  The absence of a large SCC means that all
    three strategies produce merge orders of comparable quality, and
    bisimulation shrinking erases any remaining differences between them.

    \paragraph{Results: \texttt{drones-horndl} p01.}

    The \texttt{drones-horndl} domain contains 1{,}128 variables and
    2{,}279 fact pairs.  Blind A* finds the optimal plan of cost~6
    ($h^* = 6$) by expanding 467 states in 0.075\,s.

    \textbf{All three M\&S strategies are skipped.}  With 1{,}128
    variables, the main merge loop requires $\sim$1{,}127 iterations,
    each performing label reduction over all live transition systems.
    The $O(n^2)$ scaling makes this intractable regardless of SCC
    structure — the variable count alone exceeds the practical M\&S
    limit by nearly an order of magnitude (cf.\ \texttt{vta-horndl}
    at 372 vars, which was already intractable due to a 203-variable
    SCC).

    \paragraph{Results: \texttt{ged1-ds1} and \texttt{ged1-ds2nd}.}

    The \texttt{ged1-ds1} domain (215 variables, 430 fact pairs) models genome
    edit distance; derived variables encode reachability predicates over gene
    segments.  Blind A* finds the optimal plan of cost~3 ($h^* = 3$, plan
    length~2) by expanding 22 states in 0.007\,s.

    \textbf{All three M\&S strategies are skipped.}  During SCCS
    initialization, the axiom factor phase completes 65 trivial factors
    (vars 0--19 with $n = 0$, 93~relevant ops each; vars 20--64 with
    $n = 0$, 0~relevant ops each) but hangs indefinitely on derived
    variable~65, which has $n = 9$ primary vars and \textbf{7\,434 relevant
    ops}.  The BFS product-space construction over 9 possibly multi-valued
    primary variables, combined with enumerating 7\,434 operators at each
    state, is computationally intractable.  The process was killed after
    several minutes with no progress.

    \textbf{A fourth failure mode: axiom factor BFS intractability.}
    This is distinct from the three previously documented failure modes:
    the fast-path skip threshold ($n > 25$ primary vars) does not fire
    because $n = 9 < 25$.  That threshold guards against large BFS
    \emph{state spaces} driven by many primary variables, but not against
    \emph{operator density}: 7\,434 operators per BFS state can make the
    exploration infeasible even when $n$ is small.

    \textbf{Fix: pre-BFS work-estimate guard} (implemented in
    \texttt{fts\_factory.cc}).  Before starting the BFS, compute the
    estimated work as
    \[
      W = \min(\text{product\_domain},\; \text{max\_states}) \times
          |\text{relevant\_ops}|,
    \]
    where $\text{max\_states} = 100{,}000$ is the existing state-count cap.
    If $W > 5 \times 10^8$ (500\,M), return $-1$ immediately without
    running the BFS.  Returning $-1$ is always safe: the caller omits the
    axiom factor and the heuristic remains admissible (just less tight for
    that derived variable).

    Calibration:
    \begin{itemize}
      \item \texttt{sokoban-axioms}: $n=3$, binary vars, $K=702$ ops
            $\Rightarrow W = 8 \times 702 = 5{,}616 \ll 5 \times 10^8$ —
            factor is kept (correct).
      \item \texttt{ged1-ds1}: $n=9$, multi-valued vars, $K=7{,}434$ ops
            $\Rightarrow W \geq 100{,}000 \times 7{,}434 = 744\,\text{M}
            > 5 \times 10^8$ — factor is skipped immediately (correct).
    \end{itemize}
    The guard also absorbs the $n = 0$ case trivially: with no primary
    variables the product domain is 1, so $W = 1 \times K$ is never large.

    \textbf{SCCS run confirms both failure modes.}  With the fix applied,
    the axiom factor phase finishes at $t \approx 22.7$\,s: all 120 derived
    variables (vars 65--184) are skipped via the pre-BFS guard, printing
    \emph{``estimated work $\sim$744M exceeds limit''} for each.  The main
    merge loop then begins and immediately reveals a second intractability:
    the SCC decomposition yields 65 singleton SCCs (vars 0--64, the primary
    variables) and one \textbf{giant SCC of $\sim$150 variables} (vars
    65--214, the derived variables plus their dependency closure).  After
    the first merge step \texttt{(185, 200)} at $t \approx 22.99$\,s the
    run stalls indefinitely, killed by the user.  This is the same
    $O(n^2)$ label-reduction intractability as \texttt{vta-horndl}
    (failure mode~1).  Both failure modes are therefore active in
    \texttt{ged1-ds1}: failure mode~4 (axiom BFS, now mitigated) and
    failure mode~1 (giant SCC in main loop, fundamental).

    \textbf{\texttt{ged1-ds2nd} is a different (much smaller) instance.}
    Blind A* solves it with $h^* = 1$ (plan length~1) in 0.002\,s using
    only 45 variables and 90 fact pairs.  This is roughly one fifth the
    size of \texttt{ged1-ds1} and well within M\&S tractability bounds.

    All three strategies complete successfully:
    \begin{itemize}
      \item \textbf{SCCS}: $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 1 = h^*$, 2 expanded states,
            max abs 204, 15.7\,MB, build time 0.16\,s.
      \item \textbf{stateless DFP}: $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 1 = h^*$, 2 states,
            max abs 204, 15.3\,MB, build time 0.14\,s.
      \item \textbf{stateless MIASM}: $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 1 = h^*$, 2 states,
            max abs 206, 17.7\,MB, build time 11.3\,s.
    \end{itemize}
    All three strategies produce a perfect heuristic ($h_{\mathrm{init}} = h^*$),
    expanding only 2 states.  MIASM is $\sim$75$\times$ slower to build
    than SCCS or DFP but delivers no additional quality, consistent with
    the pattern observed in \texttt{elevator-horndl}: when the problem is
    small and the heuristic already tight, MIASM's overhead is not
    justified.

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    \paragraph{Results: \texttt{psr-large}.}

    The \texttt{psr-large} domain (30 variables, 60 fact pairs) models power
    system routing; derived variables encode circuit-level reachability.
    Blind A* finds the optimal plan of cost~6 (plan length~6) by expanding
    90 states in 0.004\,s.

    The SCC decomposition yields one large SCC of 19 variables
    (\texttt{vars 0--18}) and 11 singleton SCCs (\texttt{vars 19--29}).
    The 19-variable SCC forces the bisimulation to work on a large product
    space; all three strategies hit the \texttt{max\_states} ceiling of
    50\,000 and must return a lossy approximation.

    \begin{itemize}
      \item \textbf{SCCS}: $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 1$, 26 expanded states,
            max abs 48\,992, 22.3\,MB, build 0.66\,s.
      \item \textbf{Stateless DFP}: $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 1$, 25 expanded,
            max abs 48\,992, 27.7\,MB, build 1.22\,s (2$\times$ slower
            than SCCS).
      \item \textbf{Stateless MIASM}: $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 1$, 24 expanded,
            max abs 49\,958, 24.7\,MB, build 16.9\,s (26$\times$ slower
            than SCCS).
    \end{itemize}

    Despite $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 1$ for all strategies (same as blind),
    the M\&S heuristic guides intermediate states better: expanded states
    drop from 90 (blind) to 24--26 (M\&S).  However, the 50k ceiling
    prevents the abstraction from capturing enough distance information
    for a tight initial-state estimate.  MIASM's $26\times$ build overhead
    yields only 2 fewer expanded states compared to SCCS — not cost-effective
    for this domain.

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    \paragraph{Results: \texttt{psr-middle}.}

    The \texttt{psr-middle} domain (65 variables, 130 fact pairs) is a smaller
    variant of the same power system routing family as \texttt{psr-large}.
    Blind A* finds the optimal plan of cost~4 (plan length~4) by expanding
    18 states in 0.003\,s.

    The SCC decomposition yields a 19-variable SCC (\texttt{vars 0--18})
    and four 5-variable SCCs plus singletons.  Unlike \texttt{psr-large},
    SCCS stays below the 50\,000-state ceiling (max abs 27\,318), allowing
    it to record tighter distance information and raise $h_{\mathrm{init}}$
    from 1 to~2.

    \begin{itemize}
      \item \textbf{SCCS}: $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 2$, 13 expanded states,
            max abs 27\,318, 28.9\,MB, build 0.50\,s.
      \item \textbf{Stateless DFP}: $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 2$, 9 expanded
            (best), max abs 48\,294, 32.5\,MB, build 0.91\,s
            (1.8$\times$ slower than SCCS).
      \item \textbf{Stateless MIASM}: $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 2$, 13 expanded,
            max abs 49\,886, 40.0\,MB, build 71.3\,s (143$\times$ slower
            than SCCS).
    \end{itemize}

    DFP is the standout performer: its merge order preserves tighter
    $h$-values and reduces expanded states from 13 (SCCS/MIASM) to~9,
    while staying below the 50k ceiling.  MIASM matches SCCS in search
    quality but takes 143$\times$ longer to build — not cost-effective
    for this domain.  Compared with \texttt{psr-large}, the more distributed
    SCC structure of \texttt{psr-middle} allows SCCS to avoid the ceiling,
    yielding a better $h_{\mathrm{init}}$ despite a larger variable count.

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    \paragraph{Results: \texttt{robot-horndl}.}

    The \texttt{robot-horndl} domain (38 variables, 76 fact pairs) models a
    robot navigating a grid; derived variables encode reachability and
    adjacency relations via Datalog-style axioms.
    Blind A* finds the optimal plan of cost~3 by expanding 7 states in
    0.002\,s.

    The SCC decomposition yields one large SCC of 37 variables
    (\texttt{vars 0--36}) and one singleton (\texttt{var 37}).
    Despite the 37-variable SCC, exact bisimulation collapses the product
    space to at most \textbf{6 abstract states} — the tightest abstraction
    seen in any Phase~2 domain.  All three merge strategies produce
    identical abstractions and identical search behaviour.

    \begin{itemize}
      \item \textbf{SCCS}: $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 2$, 4 expanded states,
            max abs 6, 13.1\,MB, build 0.019\,s.
      \item \textbf{Stateless DFP}: $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 2$, 4 expanded,
            max abs 6, 13.1\,MB, build 0.012\,s (1.6$\times$ faster
            than SCCS).
      \item \textbf{Stateless MIASM}: $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 2$, 4 expanded,
            max abs 6, 13.3\,MB, build 0.027\,s (2.3$\times$ slower
            than DFP).
    \end{itemize}

    All strategies achieve $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 2$ (blind: 1) and reduce
    expanded states from 7 to~4.  Because the abstraction collapses to
    only 6 states regardless of merge order, MIASM's overhead is negligible
    (2$\times$ vs DFP, compared with 143$\times$ on \texttt{psr-middle}).
    The 17 axiom factors are all small (1--7 primary vars, 2--4 ops) and
    are processed quickly without triggering the work-estimate guard.

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    \paragraph{Results: \texttt{robotConj-horndl}.}

    The \texttt{robotConj-horndl} domain (29 variables, 58 fact pairs) is
    a conjunctive variant of \texttt{robot-horndl}, with fewer variables
    (29 vs.\ 38) but the same axiom structure and optimal plan cost~3.
    Blind A* expands 7 states in 0.003\,s.

    The SCC decomposition yields one 25-variable SCC
    (\texttt{vars 0--24} by causal-graph numbering) and four singletons
    (\texttt{vars 25--28}).  Twelve axiom factors are built (vs.\ 17 for
    \texttt{robot-horndl}), all small (1--7 primary vars, 2--4 ops).
    Bisimulation collapses the product space to at most 12 abstract states
    — slightly larger than \texttt{robot-horndl}'s~6, but still tiny.
    All three merge strategies produce identical abstractions.

    \begin{itemize}
      \item \textbf{SCCS}: $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 2$, 4 expanded states,
            max abs 12, 13.0\,MB, build 0.015\,s.
      \item \textbf{Stateless DFP}: $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 2$, 4 expanded,
            max abs 12, 13.0\,MB, build 0.011\,s (1.4$\times$ faster
            than SCCS).
      \item \textbf{Stateless MIASM}: $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 2$, 4 expanded,
            max abs 12, 13.1\,MB, build 0.011\,s (same as DFP).
    \end{itemize}

    As with \texttt{robot-horndl}, all strategies achieve
    $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 2$ and 4 expanded states (vs.\ blind's 7).
    Notably, MIASM is no slower than DFP here — its overhead is
    negligible when the abstraction collapses to only 12 states.
    This confirms that MIASM's large overhead seen on \texttt{psr-middle}
    (143$\times$) is driven by the abstraction size, not by the domain
    size alone.

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    \paragraph{Results: \texttt{taskassign-horndl}.}

    The \texttt{taskassign-horndl} domain (116 variables, 233 fact pairs)
    models a task-assignment scheduling problem whose derived variables
    encode datalog-style reachability predicates.  Blind A* expands 6
    states in 0.003\,s with $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 1$.

    The SCC decomposition yields one dominant 60-variable SCC and a small
    number of singletons.  Seven axiom factors are built: the first
    operates on 0 primary variables (all derived, immediately collapsed),
    and the most expensive one (\texttt{var 102}) involves 13 primary
    variables and 25 relevant operators, accounting for about 22\,ms of
    BFS time.  Label reduction on the 60-variable SCC dominates total
    build time for all strategies.

    \begin{itemize}
      \item \textbf{SCCS (topological)}: $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 2 = h^*$,
            3 expanded states, max abs 245, 15.7\,MB, build 0.679\,s.
      \item \textbf{Stateless DFP}: $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 2 = h^*$,
            3 expanded, max abs 335, 15.4\,MB, build 0.262\,s
            (2.6$\times$ faster than SCCS).
      \item \textbf{Stateless MIASM}: $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 2 = h^*$,
            3 expanded, max abs 245, 17.2\,MB, build 2.919\,s
            (11$\times$ slower than DFP, 4.3$\times$ slower than SCCS).
    \end{itemize}

    All three strategies achieve the optimal heuristic value
    ($h_{\mathrm{init}} = 2 = h^*$) and expand only 3 states (vs.\
    blind's 6), confirming that the axiom factor construction is
    effective for this domain.  DFP is notably faster than SCCS
    (2.6$\times$) because its stateless merge ordering happens to reduce
    the label-reduction work on the large SCC.  MIASM incurs 11$\times$
    overhead relative to DFP with no gain in heuristic quality, making it
    cost-ineffective here.  The MIASM overhead scales with abstraction
    size (max abs 245--335 here vs.\ only 6--12 for the robot domains),
    consistent with the pattern observed on \texttt{psr-middle}.

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    \paragraph{Results: \texttt{word-rooms}.}

    The \texttt{word-rooms} domain (431 variables, epistemic planning;
    derived variables encode \texttt{letter-state} predicates with 23
    possible values) is the largest domain in the Phase~2 test suite.
    Blind A* expands 7{,}782 states in 0.507\,s with $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 1$.
    The optimal cost is $h^* = 9$.  The domain has over 80 goal-derived
    variables whose $S_d$ closures mutually overlap, exposing a soundness
    problem in the per-variable factor construction that required the fix
    described in the next section.

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    \paragraph{Joint axiom factor construction: soundness fix for overlapping
      $S_d$ sets.}
    \label{sec:joint-factor}

    \textbf{The soundness problem.}
    The per-variable design builds one factor $\Theta_{S_d,d}$ per
    goal-derived variable $d$.  This is sound when every pair of
    goal-derived variables has \emph{disjoint} primary-variable closures:
    each factor encodes a distinct subset of primary variables, so the
    merged FTS has no duplicate encodings.

    When two goal-derived variables $d_1$ and $d_2$ share at least one
    primary variable (i.e.\ $S_{d_1} \cap S_{d_2} \neq \emptyset$), the
    two separate factors both contain that primary variable.  In the merged
    FTS a product state $(s_1, s_2)$ with $s_1 \in \Theta_{S_{d_1},d_1}$
    and $s_2 \in \Theta_{S_{d_2},d_2}$ can assign \emph{conflicting} values
    to the shared variable: value $a$ in $s_1$ and value $b \neq a$ in
    $s_2$.  Such states are unreachable in the concrete planning problem
    (a variable holds one value at a time), but the abstract FTS treats them
    as real, introducing spurious transitions that can shorten or lengthen
    the abstract goal distance relative to the concrete optimum.  The
    heuristic is therefore no longer guaranteed to be admissible.

    \textbf{Detection.}  This bug was exposed by \texttt{word-rooms}:
    with 80$+$ goal-derived variables whose $S_d$ closures mutually
    overlap, the per-variable implementation produced incorrect heuristic
    values; the joint-factor fix below restored correctness.

    \textbf{Fix: union-find grouping.}
    Before building any axiom factors, all goal-derived variables are
    partitioned into \emph{overlap-connected groups} using a two-phase
    procedure implemented in \texttt{merge\_and\_shrink\_algorithm.cc}:

    \begin{enumerate}
      \item \textbf{Compute primary-variable closures (new free function).}
        A new function
\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++]
unordered_set<int> compute_axiom_factor_primary_vars(
    const TaskProxy &task_proxy, int derived_var_id);
\end{lstlisting}
        is declared in \texttt{fts\_factory.h} and defined in
        \texttt{fts\_factory.cc}.  It performs a BFS over the axiom rules
        to find the full primary-variable closure $S_d$ of a single derived
        variable: the queue is seeded with \texttt{derived\_var\_id}; for
        each axiom that \emph{produces} a variable already in the visited
        set, all of that axiom's primary-variable preconditions are added
        to the result set and all derived-variable preconditions are added
        to the BFS queue.  The result is the exact set $S_d$.

      \item \textbf{Union-find grouping.}
        A union-find structure is initialised with one component per
        goal-derived variable.  For every ordered pair $(d_1, d_2)$ of
        goal-derived variables, if $S_{d_1} \cap S_{d_2} \neq \emptyset$
        (checked by iterating over $S_{d_1}$ and testing membership in
        $S_{d_2}$) the two components are merged.  After all merges,
        each resulting component is a joint group whose members are built
        into a single factor.
    \end{enumerate}

    \textbf{Updated \texttt{build\_axiom\_factor} signature.}
    The function signature in \texttt{fts\_factory.h/.cc} was changed from a
    single derived variable to a group vector:
\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++]
// Old (per variable, single derived var):
int build_axiom_factor(
    const TaskProxy &task_proxy,
    int derived_var_id,
    FactoredTransitionSystem &fts,
    utils::LogProxy &log);

// New (per group, with optional pending-variable output):
int build_axiom_factor(
    const TaskProxy &task_proxy,
    const vector<int> &derived_var_ids,
    FactoredTransitionSystem &fts,
    utils::LogProxy &log,
    vector<int> *out_pending_var_order = nullptr,
    vector<vector<int>> *out_state_pending_values = nullptr);
\end{lstlisting}

    The BFS is seeded with \emph{all} members of \texttt{derived\_var\_ids}
    simultaneously (one queue entry per group member).  The product state
    space is $\prod_{p \in S} D_p$ where $S = \bigcup_{d \in \text{group}}
    S_d$.  Goal states are those in which \emph{every} member $d$ of the
    group reaches its required goal value given the primary-variable
    assignment of the current product state.

    \textbf{Pending variable tracking.}
    When a joint axiom factor is built, its primary variables are already
    encoded in the factor but the corresponding \emph{atomic} factors for
    those same variables have not yet been merged in.  Until that merge
    happens the two copies of each shared primary variable must not be
    lossily collapsed by shrinking, otherwise the axiom factor loses
    information needed to distinguish states after the merge.  Three member
    variables of \texttt{MergeAndShrinkAlgorithm} (declared in
    \texttt{merge\_and\_shrink\_algorithm.h}) track this constraint:

    \begin{itemize}
      \item \texttt{axiom\_factor\_pending\_vars[i]}: the set of primary
        variable indices that factor $i$ shares with atomic factors not yet
        merged into it.
      \item \texttt{axiom\_factor\_pending\_var\_order[i]}: the same
        variables as an ordered list; determines the column order of the
        value table below.
      \item \texttt{axiom\_factor\_pending\_values[i]}: for each abstract
        state of factor $i$, the tuple of concrete values of the pending
        primary variables.  When shrinking is requested for factor $i$
        before all pending merges are done, the equivalence-relation
        computation is replaced by
        \texttt{compute\_equivalence\_relation\_with\_seed}, which only
        collapses states that agree on every pending variable value.
    \end{itemize}

    Once the atomic factor for a pending variable is merged into the axiom
    factor, that variable is removed from the pending set.  Subsequent
    shrinking of the (now-merged) factor uses normal bisimulation without
    the seed constraint, so the overhead is incurred only during the
    transitional period.

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    \paragraph{Updated results: \texttt{word-rooms} (joint-factor fix).}
    \label{par:word-rooms}

    The SCC decomposition yields three large SCCs (136, 72, and 72
    variables respectively) plus many singletons.  The domain has over
    80 goal-derived variables whose $S_d$ closures overlap; the union-find
    grouping step (see Section~\ref{sec:joint-factor}) merges them into
    two joint axiom factors, each covering roughly 40 derived variables
    and 28 primary variables with 12 relevant operators.  Neither factor
    exceeds the 100{,}000-state BFS cap, so both are built successfully;
    the axiom phase takes $\approx 114$\,s.

    \begin{itemize}
      \item \textbf{SCCS (topological)}: $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 2$,
            2{,}035 expanded (vs.\ blind's 7{,}782; 74\% reduction),
            build 114.5\,s (total, including axiom phase), search 0.18\,s.
      \item \textbf{Stateless DFP}: $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 2$,
            2{,}035 expanded, max abs 16, peak memory 23.2\,MB,
            build 88.9\,s (26\,s \emph{faster} than SCCS; 0.2\,s search).
      \item \textbf{Stateless MIASM}: impractical.  Each call to
            compute the next merge pair takes 24--25\,s due to MIASM's
            internal simulate-and-score loop over all candidate pairs.
            With $\approx 114$\,s for the axiom phase and $\approx 25$\,s
            per merge step for 430$+$ required merges, the projected total
            exceeds 3 hours; the run was stopped after 22 minutes with
            only 49 merges completed.
    \end{itemize}

    Several observations distinguish \texttt{word-rooms} from the other
    Phase~2 domains.  First, heuristic quality is poor: $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 2$
    against $h^* = 9$ (22\% informedness), versus $h^* = h_{\mathrm{init}}$
    on \texttt{taskassign-horndl} and near-optimal on the robot domains.
    The three large SCCs force bisimulation to approximate aggressively,
    yet still fail to capture the domain's epistemic structure.  Second,
    the joint-factor fix reduces total build time from $\approx 371$\,s to
    $\approx 115$\,s for SCCS ($3.2\times$ speedup) and $\approx 89$\,s for
    DFP ($4.7\times$ speedup), mainly because grouping 80$+$ individual
    per-variable BFS runs into two joint BFS runs eliminates redundant
    reachability work over shared primary variables.  Third, the joint-factor
    fix \emph{reverses} the DFP vs.\ SCCS ordering: pre-fix, DFP was 44\,s
    \emph{slower} than SCCS (due to stateless-ordering overhead on large SCCs);
    post-fix, DFP is 26\,s \emph{faster} (max abs 16 vs.\ SCCS's larger
    intermediate abstractions), showing that DFP's transition-reducing merge
    selection is more sensitive to the structure of the resulting factor graph.
    Fourth, MIASM's per-step scoring cost makes it completely
    cost-ineffective for any domain with hundreds of variables.
    Overall, M\&S is cost-ineffective for \texttt{word-rooms}: neither
    heuristic quality nor search reduction justifies the build time
    (89--115\,s), even though the gigabyte memory cost is eliminated by the
    joint-factor fix.

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    \paragraph{Results: \texttt{philosophers} p01.}

    The \texttt{philosophers} domain models the dining-philosophers problem
    with message-passing queues; derived predicates encode the
    \texttt{blocked} and \texttt{has-fork} state of each philosopher.
    The p01 instance has 2 philosophers, 37 variables (2 derived, jointly
    encoded), and optimal cost $h^* = 18$.
    Blind A* expands 117 states in 0.77\,ms with $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 1$.

    The joint-factor BFS groups the two derived variables (31 and 36) into
    a single axiom factor with 10 primary variables and 34 relevant
    operators.

    \begin{center}
    \begin{tabular}{lrrrrrr}
    \toprule
    \textbf{Config} & \textbf{$h_{\mathrm{init}}$} & \textbf{States}
      & \textbf{Plan cost} & \textbf{Max abs.} & \textbf{Peak mem.} & \textbf{Build time} \\
    \midrule
    \texttt{blind}           & 1  & 117 & \textbf{18\,\checkmark} & --     & 12.6\,MB & 0.004\,s \\
    \texttt{SCCS}            & 10 &  58 & \textbf{18\,\checkmark} & 3\,640 & 13.4\,MB & 0.038\,s \\
    \texttt{stateless DFP}   & $18 = h^*$ & 19 & \textbf{18\,\checkmark} & 49\,959 & 20.2\,MB & 2.16\,s \\
    \texttt{stateless MIASM} & $18 = h^*$ & 19 & \textbf{18\,\checkmark} & 21\,285 & 16.6\,MB & 22.4\,s \\
    \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \end{center}

    SCCS reduces expanded states by $2\times$ over blind ($h_{\mathrm{init}}
    = 10$), while both DFP and MIASM achieve the \emph{perfect} heuristic
    ($h_{\mathrm{init}} = 18 = h^*$): the search traverses the optimal path
    directly, expanding only 19 states (vs.\ 117 blind) with $h$ decreasing
    by exactly~1 at each step.  The quality advantage of DFP over SCCS comes
    at a cost of $57\times$ slower build time; MIASM matches DFP's heuristic
    quality but is $10\times$ slower than DFP (22.4\,s vs.\ 2.16\,s) and
    produces a smaller maximum intermediate abstraction (21\,285 vs.\
    49\,959), reflecting its cautious size-minimizing merge selection.
    For this domain, DFP is the best strategy overall: it achieves the
    perfect heuristic at a build cost that is still negligible relative to
    search.

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    \paragraph{Results: \texttt{optical-telegraphs}.}

    The \texttt{optical-telegraphs} domain models asynchronous message
    passing via optical telegraph stations with derived predicates encoding
    reachability conditions over station states.
    The test instance has 177~variables (4~derived: vars~161, 162, 169, 176)
    and optimal cost $h^* = 28$.
    Blind A* expands 10{,}179 states with $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 1$.

    All four derived variables share overlapping support sets and are grouped
    by union-find into a single joint axiom factor with 42~primary variables
    and 286~relevant operators.
    The BFS over this factor's product space exceeds the 100{,}000-state cap
    and is \emph{skipped}; the heuristic consequently returns
    $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 0$ for every goal condition involving derived
    variables, giving M\&S no useful guidance.

    \begin{center}
    \begin{tabular}{lrrrrrr}
    \toprule
    \textbf{Config} & \textbf{$h_{\mathrm{init}}$} & \textbf{States}
      & \textbf{Plan cost} & \textbf{Max abs.} & \textbf{Peak mem.} & \textbf{Build time} \\
    \midrule
    \texttt{blind}           & 1 & 10{,}179 & \textbf{28\,\checkmark} & --          & 13.5\,MB & -- \\
    \texttt{SCCS}            & 0 & 10{,}662 & \textbf{28\,\checkmark} & 49{,}892    & 39.3\,MB & 16.3\,s \\
    \texttt{stateless DFP}   & 0 & 10{,}662 & \textbf{28\,\checkmark} & 49{,}892    & 39.2\,MB & 17.6\,s \\
    \texttt{stateless MIASM} & 0 & 10{,}662 & \textbf{28\,\checkmark} & 49{,}185    & 45.3\,MB & 195\,s \\
    \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \end{center}

    All three M\&S strategies produce $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 0$ and expand
    10{,}662~states---5\% more than blind A*'s 10{,}179---while incurring
    build times of 16--195\,s and peak memory of 39--45\,MB versus 13.5\,MB
    for blind.
    The axiom factor's product BFS is the sole culprit: with 42~primary
    variables and 286~relevant operators the 100{,}000-state cap is hit,
    the factor is skipped, and all derived-variable goal conditions receive
    heuristic value~0.
    MIASM's 195\,s build time makes it far worse than SCCS or DFP on this
    domain with no quality gain to show for it.
    For \texttt{optical-telegraphs}, M\&S is completely cost-ineffective:
    the heuristic provides no search guidance and no strategy justifies
    the build overhead.

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    \paragraph{Results: \texttt{muddy-child}.}

    The \texttt{muddy-child/3-muddy\_1.sas} instance encodes a 3-child muddy
    children puzzle with 44~variables (8~derived: vars~36--43) and optimal
    cost $h^* = 4$.
    Blind A* expands 14 states with $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 0$.

    The 8~derived variables split into five union-find groups.
    Four singleton groups (vars~37, 38, 41, 42) each produce a trivial axiom
    factor with 0~primary variables and 0~relevant operators; these factors
    are 1-state transition systems contributing no heuristic information.
    The fifth group (vars~36, 39, 40, 43) produces a joint axiom factor with
    12~primary variables and 4~relevant operators.
    During BFS goal-state evaluation, derived variable conditions are
    over-approximated: any BFS state whose non-$S_d$ goal conditions cannot be
    verified without a full axiom evaluation is treated as a candidate, but the
    actual axiom evaluator then finds no state satisfies all four derived goal
    conditions.
    The factor therefore has \emph{zero goal states}.

    Prior to Fix~A (\texttt{build\_axiom\_factor} returning~$-1$ when no goal
    state is found), this caused a crash under \texttt{SCCS}: the empty
    group~0 in \texttt{ShrinkBisimulation::initialize\_groups} propagated into
    \texttt{apply\_abstraction}, which asserts every equivalence class is
    non-empty.
    Fix~A skips the factor and logs a diagnostic message, preserving
    admissibility at the cost of losing all heuristic information about
    the derived goal variables.

    \begin{center}
    \begin{tabular}{lrrrrrr}
    \toprule
    \textbf{Config} & \textbf{$h_{\mathrm{init}}$} & \textbf{States}
      & \textbf{Plan cost} & \textbf{Max abs.} & \textbf{Peak mem.} & \textbf{Build time} \\
    \midrule
    \texttt{blind}           & 0 & 14 & \textbf{4\,\checkmark} & --  & $\approx$13.0\,MB & -- \\
    \texttt{SCCS}            & 0 & 20 & \textbf{4\,\checkmark} & 2   & 12.8\,MB & 18.9\,ms \\
    \texttt{stateless DFP}   & 0 & 20 & \textbf{4\,\checkmark} & 2   & 12.8\,MB & 24.3\,ms \\
    \texttt{stateless MIASM} & 0 & 20 & \textbf{4\,\checkmark} & 2   & 12.9\,MB & 49.7\,ms \\
    \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \end{center}

    All three M\&S strategies produce $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 0$ and expand
    20~states---43\% more than blind A*'s 14---while incurring build times
    of 19--50\,ms with negligible memory overhead.
    The maximum intermediate abstraction size of~2 confirms that no useful
    abstraction is constructed: the only axiom factor with semantic content
    (the joint factor for vars~36, 39, 40, 43) is discarded, and the
    remaining singleton factors contribute nothing beyond 1-state systems.
    Fix~A therefore introduces a correctness--informativeness trade-off:
    the heuristic is admissible but blind with respect to all derived goal
    conditions.
    For \texttt{muddy-child}, M\&S provides no search guidance and blind A*
    is strictly preferable.

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    \paragraph{Results: \texttt{muddy-children}.}

    The \texttt{muddy-children/3-muddy.sas} instance encodes a larger 3-child
    muddy children puzzle with 105~variables (24~derived: vars~81--104) and
    optimal cost $h^* = 6$.
    Blind A* expands 35~states with $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 1$ (the goal
    conditions include primary variable constraints, so the blind heuristic
    returns~1 for the non-goal initial state).

    The 24~derived variables split into five union-find groups.
    Four singleton groups (vars~88, 90, 93, 95) each produce an axiom factor
    with 4~primary variables and 3~relevant operators whose BFS finds no goal
    states; these factors are skipped, preserving admissibility.
    The fifth joint group (vars~81--104, excluding the four singletons above)
    produces an axiom factor with 41~primary variables, 4~relevant operators,
    10~reachable states, and 1~goal state, yielding $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 2$.

    \textbf{Code fixes required.}
    Before the fixes described below, unmodified M\&S crashed and later
    declared the instance unsolvable; multiple independent bugs had to be
    corrected in concert before the three strategies returned the right answer.

    \emph{Fix~A — skip axiom factors whose BFS finds no goal states}
    (\texttt{fts\_factory.cc}).
    Applies to the four singleton groups (vars~88, 90, 93, 95).
    Each runs a BFS over 4~primary variables using 3~relevant operators, but
    no state passes the full axiom-closure check, leaving the goal-state vector
    empty.
    Without Fix~A \texttt{build\_axiom\_factor} returned a zero-goal-state
    \texttt{TransitionSystem}, and the subsequent
    \texttt{ShrinkBisimulation::initialize\_groups} call asserted that
    group~0 was non-empty — a crash.
    Fix~A makes \texttt{build\_axiom\_factor} return~$-1$ before constructing
    the transition system; the caller skips the factor entirely.
    (This fix is shared with \texttt{muddy-child}; see that paragraph for
    the detailed crash trace.)

    \emph{Fix~B --- skip unreachable-state pruning for derived-variable atomic factors}
    (\texttt{merge\_and\_shrink\_algorithm.cc}, initial pruning loop).
    The M\&S algorithm prunes atomic factors before the main merge loop.
    Unreachable-state pruning works by forward BFS from the initial state
    using operator transitions.
    For a derived variable such as \texttt{muddy(child\_a)}, the value in the
    initial state is the default axiom value (usually~0); the variable is
    updated by axiom rules, \emph{not} operators, so operators cannot reach
    value~1 from value~0 in the derived variable's atomic factor.
    Forward pruning therefore labels the non-default value as unreachable
    and removes it, producing a 1-state factor that is blind to the goal
    condition on the derived variable.
    The fix introduces an \texttt{is\_derived\_atomic} flag (true when
    \texttt{index $<$ num\_variables} and the variable is derived) and
    disables \texttt{prune\_unreachable\_states} for those factors.

    \emph{Fix~C --- skip irrelevant-state pruning for axiom factors}
    (\texttt{merge\_and\_shrink\_algorithm.cc}, initial pruning loop).
    Irrelevant-state pruning works by backward BFS from goal states.
    An axiom factor (index $\geq$ \texttt{num\_variables}) covers only a
    subset of the primary variables in $S_d$; backward reachability within
    that partial space does not capture all operator paths that lead to a
    goal in the full system, so states that are genuinely reachable via
    operators outside $S_d$ can be incorrectly labelled irrelevant and
    pruned, yielding $h = \infty$ for concrete states that map to them.
    The fix introduces an \texttt{is\_axiom\_factor} flag and disables
    \texttt{prune\_irrelevant\_states} for those factors.
    A companion guard on the solvability check prevents the
    algorithm from declaring the whole problem unsolvable if an axiom
    factor appears unsolvable after its (legitimately constrained) pruning
    pass.

    \emph{Fix~D --- skip unreachable/irrelevant pruning for axiom-derived merged factors}
    (\texttt{merge\_and\_shrink\_algorithm.cc}, main merge loop).
    During the merge loop a boolean array \texttt{is\_axiom\_derived} tracks
    whether a factor descends from any axiom factor.
    The same reasoning as Fixes~B--C applies to merged products that contain
    an axiom factor: applying either pruning type to such a product could
    remove states that are reachable in the full system via operators not
    represented in the partial product.
    The fix propagates the flag across merges
    (\texttt{is\_axiom\_derived[new] = is\_axiom\_derived[left] || is\_axiom\_derived[right]})
    and skips both pruning types and the solvability check for any merged
    factor that carries the flag.

    \emph{Fix~E --- collapse dead-end abstract states in the axiom factor}
    (\texttt{merge\_and\_shrink\_algorithm.cc}).
    After the joint axiom factor (vars~81--104) is added to the FTS and
    goal distances are computed, some BFS-reachable abstract states may have
    goal distance~$\infty$ (they are dead ends in the abstract space).
    A concrete state that maps to such an abstract state would receive
    $h = \infty$, making the heuristic inadmissible.
    The fix constructs a \texttt{StateEquivalenceRelation} that merges every
    dead-end abstract state into the goal abstract state (keeping non-dead-end
    states in their own singleton classes) and calls
    \texttt{fts.apply\_abstraction}.
    Because the goal state has $h = 0 \leq h^*$, lumping dead-end states into
    it is admissible.

    \emph{Fix~F --- collapse derived-variable atomic factors to a single abstract state}
    (\texttt{merge\_and\_shrink\_algorithm.cc}).
    Even with Fixes~A--E, the derived-variable atomic factors
    (indices 81--104 in the FTS) can cause $h = \infty$ during later
    merge-loop shrinking: their transition systems have no useful structure
    (they are set by axiom rules, not operators), but bisimulation-shrinking
    can still produce an abstract goal distance of~$\infty$ for the initial
    abstract state if no goal-reaching path exists within the single-variable
    factor alone.
    The fix collapses each derived atomic factor to a trivial single-state
    abstraction immediately after the axiom factor is built:
\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++]
for (int d : derived_var_ids) {
    if (fts.is_active(d)) {
        int sz = fts.get_transition_system(d).get_size();
        StateEquivalenceRelation rel(1);
        for (int s = 0; s < sz; ++s)
            rel[0].push_front(s);
        fts.apply_abstraction(d, rel, log);
    }
}
\end{lstlisting}
    This removes the factor's ability to distinguish any variable values,
    making it a neutral 1-state factor that contributes no information and
    cannot raise $h$ above zero.

    \emph{Fix~G --- fallback abstract state for primary-variable configurations absent from the BFS}
    (\texttt{fts\_factory.cc},
     \texttt{merge\_and\_shrink\_representation.h/.cc}).
    This fix resolved the final and most subtle bug: after all prior fixes,
    seven concrete task states were still reported as dead ends, causing the
    search to declare the problem unsolvable.

    \textbf{Root cause.}
