    The BFS in \texttt{build\_axiom\_factor} enqueues only operators with
    \texttt{has\_derived\_pre = false}; operators whose only connection to the
    axiom factor is a precondition on the target derived variable are skipped
    during exploration (in \texttt{fts\_factory.cc}):
\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++]
if (rop.has_derived_pre)
    continue; // applicability depends on d'; deferred to after goal states
\end{lstlisting}
    After the BFS completes, those operators receive self-loops \emph{only} at
    goal states:
\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++]
for (const RelevantOperator &rop : relevant_ops) {
    if (!rop.has_derived_pre) continue;
    for (int s = 0; s < num_reachable_states; ++s)
        if (goal_states[s])
            label_trans[rop.label].emplace_back(s, s);
}
\end{lstlisting}
    Concretely, a task operator conditioned on a derived variable is
    applicable only when that derived variable holds (i.e.\ in the goal
    abstract state of the factor) and may advance the primary variables to a
    combination that was \emph{never visited by the BFS}.
    That combination has no entry in the sparse
    \texttt{unordered\_map<long long,\,int>} lookup table of
    \texttt{MergeAndShrinkRepresentationProduct}; \texttt{get\_value} fell
    back to \texttt{PRUNED\_STATE} ($= -1$), which the distance machinery
    interprets as $h = \infty$.

    \textbf{Fix.}
    A new field \texttt{int fallback\_abstract\_id} (default $-1$ for
    non-sparse representations) is added to
    \texttt{MergeAndShrinkRepresentationProduct}.
    In \texttt{fts\_factory.cc}, the dense id of the unique goal state is
    saved \emph{before} the \texttt{goal\_states} vector is moved into
    the \texttt{TransitionSystem}:
\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++]
int goal_dense_id = -1;
for (int d = 0; d < num_reachable_states; ++d)
    if (goal_states[d]) { goal_dense_id = d; break; }
\end{lstlisting}
    This id is passed to the sparse constructor and stored as
    \texttt{fallback\_abstract\_id}.
    In \texttt{get\_value}, an absent key now returns the fallback:
\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++]
auto it = lookup_table.find(product_state_id);
return it != lookup_table.end() ? it->second
       : (fallback_abstract_id >= 0 ? fallback_abstract_id
                                     : PRUNED_STATE);
\end{lstlisting}
    The fallback is kept consistent through abstraction (remapped via
    \texttt{apply\_abstraction\_to\_lookup\_table} using the same
    \texttt{abstraction\_mapping} array as every table entry) and through
    distance-setting (\texttt{set\_distances} converts it from an abstract
    state id to a goal distance, exactly like every other live entry).
    After this fix, all seven previously-dead-end states received
    $h = 0$ (they map to the goal abstract state), the search found no
    dead ends, and the optimal plan of cost~$6$ was returned.

    \begin{center}
    \begin{tabular}{lrrrrrr}
    \toprule
    \textbf{Config} & \textbf{$h_{\mathrm{init}}$} & \textbf{States}
      & \textbf{Plan cost} & \textbf{Max abs.} & \textbf{Peak mem.} & \textbf{Build time} \\
    \midrule
    \texttt{blind}           & 1 & 35 & \textbf{6\,\checkmark} & --  & 12.7\,MB & -- \\
    \texttt{SCCS}            & 2 & 54 & \textbf{6\,\checkmark} & 10  & 13.5\,MB & 0.413\,s \\
    \texttt{stateless DFP}   & 2 & 54 & \textbf{6\,\checkmark} & 10  & 13.1\,MB & 0.124\,s \\
    \texttt{stateless MIASM} & 2 & 54 & \textbf{6\,\checkmark} & 10  & 14.8\,MB & 1.44\,s \\
    \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \end{center}

    All three M\&S strategies achieve $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 2$ and find the
    optimal plan (cost~6), but expand 54~states---54\% more than blind A*'s
    35.  The apparent paradox ($h_{\mathrm{init}}=2 > 1$ yet more states
    expanded) arises because the 10-state abstraction is too coarse: many
    non-goal concrete states map to the goal abstract state and receive
    $h = 0$.  During search these states are treated like unexplored Dijkstra
    nodes ($f = g$), while blind's $h = 1$ keeps $f = g + 1$ and defers them
    longer, yielding tighter pruning overall.
    All three strategies produce identical search performance; the only
    differences are in build time.  DFP (0.124\,s) is 3.3$\times$ faster than
    SCCS (0.413\,s) and 11.6$\times$ faster than MIASM (1.44\,s), with no
    benefit in heuristic quality.
    For \texttt{muddy-children}, M\&S provides negative search guidance and
    blind A* is strictly preferable.

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\paragraph{Domain: \texttt{sum} (\texttt{ce}).}
The \texttt{sum} benchmark (instance \texttt{sum\_3.sas}) is a knowledge-based
reasoning problem in which agents must collaboratively determine a target sum.
The translated task has 114 variables, of which 9 are derived (variables
105--113), placing it in the compiled-to-explicit axioms (\texttt{ce}) category.
All 9 derived variables belong to a single axiom factor group containing
26~primary variables, 7~relevant operators, and \textbf{28 reachable abstract
states} with exactly 1~goal state.
Unlike the \texttt{muddy-children} domain, the \texttt{sum} axiom factor builds
without error under all three merge strategies: no additional code fixes were
required beyond those already applied for \texttt{muddy-children}.

Blind A* serves as the baseline: it reports $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 1$ and
expands 5~states before finding the optimal plan of cost~3
(\texttt{all\_sense}; \texttt{all\_hear\_if\_b\_knows\_his\_number\_1};
\texttt{all\_hear\_if\_c\_knows\_his\_number\_1}).
The three M\&S configurations all produce $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 2$, a strictly
tighter admissible bound than blind's~1 (with optimal cost $h^* = 3$), and each
expands only 4~states---one fewer than blind.

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrr}
\toprule
\textbf{Config} & \textbf{$h_{\mathrm{init}}$} & \textbf{Expanded}
  & \textbf{Plan cost} & \textbf{Max abs} & \textbf{Peak mem} & \textbf{Build} \\
\midrule
blind A*            & 1 & 5 & 3 & ---  & 12.7\,MB & ---       \\
SCCS                & 2 & 4 & 3 & 28   & 14.0\,MB & 1.430\,s  \\
stateless DFP       & 2 & 4 & 3 & 28   & 13.3\,MB & 0.275\,s  \\
stateless MIASM     & 2 & 4 & 3 & 28   & 14.7\,MB & 2.176\,s  \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

All three strategies build the same 28-state axiom factor abstraction and
achieve identical search performance: $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 2$, 4~expanded
states, and the same optimal plan.  The differences lie entirely in build time.
DFP (0.275\,s) is 5.2$\times$ faster than SCCS (1.430\,s) and 7.9$\times$
faster than MIASM (2.176\,s), while delivering no advantage in heuristic
quality.  Zero dead ends are detected by any strategy.

The \texttt{sum} domain is a positive showcase for M\&S with axioms.  Because
the axiom factor captures the full derived-variable reasoning in 28 states with
a single goal state, the resulting heuristic achieves $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 2$,
improving on blind's~1 and reducing node expansions from~5 to~4.  DFP provides
the best trade-off: perfect heuristic quality at the lowest build cost.

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\paragraph{Results: \texttt{airport-adl}.}

The \texttt{airport-adl} benchmark models airport ground traffic management:
a single airplane must taxi from a runway segment to a parking position by
traversing a sequence of taxiway segments.
The translated SAS$^+$ task for the tested instance has \textbf{10 variables}
and 45 fact pairs; crucially, \emph{no derived variables} appear in the
translation.
Although the domain is listed under ``compiled axioms'' (no explicit
\texttt{:derived} in PDDL), the translator produces a purely propositional
task, so M\&S runs without any axiom-specific code paths.
The causal graph places all 10 variables in a \textbf{single SCC}.

Blind A* reports $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 1$ and expands 18~states to find the
optimal plan of cost~8 (7~\texttt{move} actions followed by
\texttt{park airplane\_cfbeg medium seg\_pp\_0\_60 south}).

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrr}
\toprule
\textbf{Config} & \textbf{$h_{\mathrm{init}}$} & \textbf{Expanded}
  & \textbf{Plan cost} & \textbf{Max abs} & \textbf{Peak mem} & \textbf{Build} \\
\midrule
blind A*            & 1 & 18 & 8 & ---  & 12.96\,MB & ---       \\
SCCS                & 8 & 9  & 8 & 56   & 12.96\,MB & 0.00219\,s  \\
stateless DFP       & 8 & 9  & 8 & 56   & 12.96\,MB & 0.00204\,s  \\
stateless MIASM     & 8 & 9  & 8 & 28   & 12.96\,MB & 0.00273\,s  \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

All three M\&S strategies achieve $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 8 = h^*$: a
\textbf{perfect heuristic} matching the optimal plan cost exactly.
Each expands only 9~states---half of blind's~18---and identifies 7~dead-end
states before expansion.
Since the causal graph is a single SCC, SCCS has no SCC structure to exploit
and degenerates to the same merge-selector as stateless DFP; accordingly both
strategies produce an identical merge order and a maximum intermediate
abstraction of 56~states.
MIASM chooses a different merge sequence and yields a smaller peak abstraction
of 28~states at the cost of a 34\,\% longer build (0.00273\,s vs.\ 0.00204\,s
for DFP), with no difference in search quality.
DFP is the fastest strategy (7\,\% faster than SCCS), while SCCS and DFP
both produce the smallest-footprint heuristic in their shared 56-state
abstraction.

Because all variables are combined into a single monolithic abstraction that
preserves exact optimal distances, the final abstraction is a perfect
bisimulation: the heuristic equals $h^*$ at every reachable state.
A* therefore expands only the 9~states along the optimal path (one per
$f$-layer from 8 down to~0), with no suboptimal backtracking.
For \texttt{airport-adl}, M\&S with any of the three strategies strictly
dominates blind A*.

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\paragraph{Results: \texttt{appn-adl}.}

The \texttt{appn-adl} benchmark models an active power-network diagnosis
problem: a breaker must be driven through a sequence of command and observation
steps to reach an abnormal state.
Like \texttt{airport-adl}, this domain carries no explicit \texttt{:derived}
predicates; the translated SAS$^+$ task has \textbf{12 variables} and 28 fact
pairs with \emph{no derived variables}.
The causal graph decomposes into \textbf{five SCCs}:
two small clusters $\{0,2\}$ and $\{8,3,6,9,4,7,5\}$, and three singletons
$\{1\}$, $\{10\}$, $\{11\}$.
The task contains zero-cost operators (\texttt{advance-to-obs}, several command
and close actions), so blind A* returns $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 0$
(the minimum operator cost) and provides no search guidance.

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrr}
\toprule
\textbf{Config} & \textbf{$h_{\mathrm{init}}$} & \textbf{Expanded}
  & \textbf{Plan cost} & \textbf{Max abs} & \textbf{Peak mem} & \textbf{Build} \\
\midrule
blind A*            & 0 & 16 & 2 & ---  & 12.83\,MB & ---       \\
SCCS                & 2 & 16 & 2 & 162  & 12.96\,MB & 0.00315\,s  \\
stateless DFP       & 2 & 16 & 2 & 162  & 12.96\,MB & 0.00268\,s  \\
stateless MIASM     & 2 & 16 & 2 & 108  & 12.96\,MB & 0.00207\,s  \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

All three M\&S strategies achieve $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 2 = h^*$: a perfect
heuristic at the initial state.
Despite this, all three expand the same 16~states as blind, and no dead ends
are detected.
The explanation lies in the zero-cost operator structure: because
\texttt{advance-to-obs} and most command actions have cost~0, the only
cost-bearing actions are the two \texttt{breaker-abnormal} steps (cost~1 each).
Under A*, the $f$-value of the initial state is $g + h = 0 + 2 = 2$, and
because every optimal-path state satisfies $g + h^* = 2$ exactly, all
reachable states on the optimal path share $f = 2$.
A* therefore cannot defer any reachable state on cost grounds: the perfect
heuristic gives $f = 2$ everywhere on the optimal path, so every such state
must be expanded, yielding the same 16~expansions as blind.

MIASM reverses its typical behaviour relative to the other domains: it is the
\emph{fastest} strategy (0.00207\,s, 29\,\% faster than DFP and 52\,\% faster
than SCCS) and simultaneously produces the \emph{smallest} peak abstraction
(108~states vs.\ 162 for SCCS and DFP), with no penalty in heuristic quality.
SCCS is the slowest despite exploiting the five-SCC decomposition, because the
large $\{8,3,6,9,4,7,5\}$ SCC still forces all seven variables to be merged
in a single group.
DFP is 15\,\% faster than SCCS.

For \texttt{appn-adl}, M\&S provides a perfect admissible heuristic
($h_{\mathrm{init}} = 2 = h^*$) but cannot reduce node expansions due to the
zero-cost action structure.
MIASM is the recommended strategy: it achieves the same quality as SCCS and
DFP at the lowest build cost and smallest memory footprint.

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\paragraph{Results: \texttt{fridge}.}

The \texttt{fridge} domain (smallest available instance) has \textbf{105
variables} and 210 fact pairs with no derived variables in the SAS$^+$
translation.
Blind A* reports $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 1$ (minimum operator cost) and is
already impractical: after 79\,s it has expanded 4.4\,M states across
$f$-layers 1--15, consuming 466\,MB, with no solution found.

M\&S testing was not attempted for the following reasons:
\begin{itemize}
  \item With 105 variables and a dense causal graph, the max\_states=50\,000
    limit is likely hit after merging only a small fraction of variables,
    yielding a near-trivial partial abstraction.
  \item The resulting heuristic would almost certainly remain at
    $h_{\mathrm{init}} \approx 1$, providing no improvement over blind.
  \item Even a perfect $h_{\mathrm{init}}$ could not rescue the search: each
    $f$-layer already contains millions of states, so any improvement in
    heuristic quality would reduce expansions by at most a constant factor
    while the search still takes minutes.
\end{itemize}

\texttt{fridge} is infeasible for practical M\&S testing at this instance
size.  The domain requires either a more compact problem encoding or a
heuristic specifically designed for domains with very large flat state spaces.

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\paragraph{Results: \texttt{openstacks}.}

The \texttt{openstacks} benchmark models a make-to-order production planning
problem: products must be manufactured and orders shipped while minimising
the number of open stacks.
The translated SAS$^+$ task has \textbf{22 variables} and 57 fact pairs with
no derived variables, and the causal graph is a \textbf{single SCC} containing
all 22 variables.
Blind A* reports $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 1$ and expands 4\,782 states in 11\,ms
to find the optimal plan of cost~23.

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrr}
\toprule
\textbf{Config} & \textbf{$h_{\mathrm{init}}$} & \textbf{Expanded}
  & \textbf{Plan cost} & \textbf{Max abs} & \textbf{Peak mem} & \textbf{Build} \\
\midrule
blind A*            &  1 & 4\,782 & 23 & ---   & 13.09\,MB & ---       \\
SCCS                & 11 & 2\,010 & 23 & 1\,680 & 13.23\,MB & 0.01775\,s  \\
stateless DFP       & 11 & 2\,010 & 23 & 1\,680 & 13.22\,MB & 0.01604\,s  \\
stateless MIASM     & 11 & 2\,010 & 23 & 2\,970 & 13.84\,MB & 0.01473\,s  \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

All three M\&S strategies achieve $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 11$, a substantial
improvement over blind's~1 (optimal cost $h^* = 23$).
All three expand exactly 2\,010 states---58\,\% fewer than blind's 4\,782---and
prune 736 dead-end states before expansion.
Because all 22 variables form a single SCC, SCCS degenerates to the same
merge selector as stateless DFP, producing an identical merge order, identical
maximum intermediate abstraction (1\,680 states), and identical search
performance; the only difference is build time: DFP (0.01604\,s) is 10\,\%
faster than SCCS (0.01775\,s).

MIASM exhibits an unusual pattern on this domain: it produces a
\emph{larger} maximum intermediate abstraction (2\,970 states vs.\ 1\,680 for
SCCS/DFP) while simultaneously being the \emph{fastest} strategy to build
(0.01473\,s, 8\,\% faster than DFP).
The larger peak abstraction reflects MIASM's merge order deferring costly
bisimulation shrinking steps until later in the sequence; despite exploring
more intermediate states, it avoids the repeated small bisimulation
computations that slow SCCS and DFP in the early merges.
MIASM also consumes slightly more peak memory (13.84\,MB vs.\ 13.22\,MB),
the trade-off for its faster build.
All three strategies produce identical heuristic quality and search behaviour.

For \texttt{openstacks}, M\&S strictly dominates blind: $h_{\mathrm{init}}$
jumps from~1 to~11, expansions drop by 58\,\%, and 736 dead ends are pruned
at no quality cost.  MIASM is the recommended strategy---fastest build with
equal search performance---unless memory is constrained, in which case DFP
offers the best time/memory balance.

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\paragraph{Results: \texttt{openstacks-opt08-adl}.}

The \texttt{openstacks-opt08-adl} domain is the IPC\,2008 optimal-track
variant of \texttt{openstacks}, translated with zero-cost operators for most
actions (\texttt{start-order}, \texttt{make-product}, \texttt{ship-order}) and
cost~1 only for \texttt{open-new-stack}.
The translated SAS$^+$ task has \textbf{21 variables} and 51 fact pairs with
no derived variables; the causal graph is a \textbf{single SCC} containing all
21 variables.
Because the minimum operator cost is~0, blind A* returns
$h_{\mathrm{init}} = 0$ and expands 209~states in under 1\,ms, finding an
optimal plan of cost~2 (17~steps, two \texttt{open-new-stack} actions).

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrr}
\toprule
\textbf{Config} & \textbf{$h_{\mathrm{init}}$} & \textbf{Expanded}
  & \textbf{Plan cost} & \textbf{Max abs} & \textbf{Peak mem} & \textbf{Build} \\
\midrule
blind A*            & 0 & 209 & 2 & ---   & 12.96\,MB & ---       \\
SCCS                & 1 & 209 & 2 & 560   & 13.10\,MB & 0.01304\,s  \\
stateless DFP       & 1 & 209 & 2 & 560   & 13.09\,MB & 0.02045\,s  \\
stateless MIASM     & 1 & 209 & 2 & 1\,060 & 13.37\,MB & 0.02508\,s  \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

All three M\&S strategies achieve $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 1$, an improvement over
blind's~0 but \emph{below} the optimal $h^* = 2$: the abstraction is lossy
and cannot recover the exact cost of both stack-opening actions from the initial
state.
Despite the heuristic improvement, all three strategies expand the same 209
states as blind and detect no dead ends.
The explanation mirrors \texttt{appn-adl}: with zero-cost operators, every
state on the optimal path satisfies $f = g + h = 2$ (once the first
\texttt{open-new-stack} fires), so A* cannot defer any reachable state and the
full 209-state search is unavoidable regardless of heuristic quality.

The build-time ordering is reversed relative to the usual pattern.
SCCS is the \emph{fastest} strategy (0.01304\,s), DFP is 57\,\% slower
(0.02045\,s), and MIASM is the slowest (0.02508\,s, 92\,\% slower than SCCS).
SCCS and DFP produce identical maximum intermediate abstractions (560~states),
while MIASM's different merge sequence yields a larger peak of 1\,060~states
and slightly more peak memory (13.37\,MB vs.\ 13.10\,MB).

For \texttt{openstacks-opt08-adl}, M\&S provides a mild heuristic improvement
($h=1$ vs.\ $h=0$) but no search benefit due to the zero-cost action structure.
Of the three strategies, SCCS is the best choice: fastest build, smallest
abstraction, and equal search quality.

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\paragraph{Results: \texttt{openstacks-sat08-adl}.}

The \texttt{openstacks-sat08-adl} domain is the IPC\,2008 satisficing-track
variant of \texttt{openstacks}.
Like \texttt{openstacks-opt08-adl}, it uses zero-cost operators for most
actions (\texttt{start-order}, \texttt{make-product}, \texttt{ship-order})
with cost~1 only for \texttt{open-new-stack}.
The translated SAS$^+$ task again has \textbf{21 variables} and 51 fact pairs,
no derived variables, and a \textbf{single SCC} spanning all 21 variables.
The instance is larger: blind A* expands 463~states and finds an optimal plan
of cost~3 (18~steps, three \texttt{open-new-stack} actions).

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrr}
\toprule
\textbf{Config} & \textbf{$h_{\mathrm{init}}$} & \textbf{Expanded}
  & \textbf{Plan cost} & \textbf{Max abs} & \textbf{Peak mem} & \textbf{Build} \\
\midrule
blind A*            & 0 & 463 & 3 & ---    & 12.96\,MB & ---       \\
SCCS                & 1 & 463 & 3 & 560    & 13.10\,MB & 0.01720\,s  \\
stateless DFP       & 1 & 463 & 3 & 560    & 13.10\,MB & 0.01324\,s  \\
stateless MIASM     & 1 & 463 & 3 & 1\,272 & 13.35\,MB & 0.02373\,s  \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

All three M\&S strategies yield $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 1$, improving over
blind's~0 but falling short of $h^* = 3$: the abstraction is lossy and cannot
represent the cost of all three stack-opening steps from the initial state.
The same zero-cost f-layer invariant seen in \texttt{appn-adl} and
\texttt{openstacks-opt08-adl} applies: once the first \texttt{open-new-stack}
fires, every remaining state on the optimal path shares $f = 3$, so no state
can be deferred and all 463 expansions are unavoidable regardless of heuristic
quality.
No dead ends are detected.

The build-time ranking differs from the \texttt{opt08} variant.
Here DFP is the \emph{fastest} strategy (0.01324\,s), SCCS is 30\,\%
slower (0.01720\,s), and MIASM is the slowest (0.02373\,s, 79\,\% slower
than DFP).
SCCS and DFP produce identical maximum intermediate abstractions (560~states),
while MIASM's merge order yields a larger peak of 1\,272~states and slightly
more peak memory (13.35\,MB vs.\ 13.10\,MB).
The reversal of the SCCS/DFP ordering relative to \texttt{opt08} reflects
instance-specific label-reduction encounters along the merge sequence rather
than a systematic property of either strategy.

For \texttt{openstacks-sat08-adl}, M\&S provides a mild heuristic gain
($h=1$ vs.\ $h=0$) but no expansion savings due to the zero-cost action
structure.
DFP offers the fastest build at equal search quality; SCCS is a close
second and produces the same smallest-abstraction result.

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\paragraph{Results: \texttt{trucks}.}

The \texttt{trucks} domain models time-constrained package delivery by trucks
across locations.
The translated SAS$^+$ task has \textbf{19 variables} and 47 fact pairs with
no derived variables.
All operators carry unit cost, so blind A* returns $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 1$
and expands 3\,513~states to find an optimal plan of cost~13 (13~steps).

The causal graph decomposes into \textbf{two SCCs}: a small one with
4~variables (indices 0--3, likely the time-step variables) and a large one
with 15~variables covering the spatial and package-transport state.

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrrr}
\toprule
\textbf{Config} & \textbf{$h_{\mathrm{init}}$} & \textbf{Expanded}
  & \textbf{Dead ends} & \textbf{Max abs} & \textbf{Peak mem} & \textbf{Build} \\
\midrule
blind A*            &  1 & 3\,513 &  0 & ---     & 13.10\,MB & ---       \\
SCCS                & 13 &     14 & 25 & 8\,192  & 14.93\,MB & 0.02250\,s  \\
stateless DFP       &  9 &    176 &  6 & 47\,898 & 43.36\,MB & 1.031\,s  \\
stateless MIASM     & 12 &     41 & 39 & 48\,590 & 32.71\,MB & 1.162\,s  \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

SCCS achieves $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 13 = h^*$, a \emph{perfect bisimulation}.
By first merging variables within each SCC in topological order, it constructs
a compact 8\,192-state abstraction that preserves all optimal distances.
The search stays entirely within the $f = 13$ layer---every state on the
optimal path satisfies $g + h = 13$---and expands only 14~states (one per
plan step plus the goal), a 99.6\,\% reduction over blind.
The 25~dead ends detected are genuine infeasible states, correctly identified
thanks to the perfect heuristic.
The build completes in 22\,ms with modest memory (14.93\,MB).

Stateless DFP and MIASM, which ignore SCC structure, pay a steep penalty.
Both grow abstractions approaching the 50\,000-state limit (47\,898 and
48\,590~states) and require over 1\,s to build while consuming 2--3$\times$
more memory than SCCS.
Despite this cost, neither recovers a perfect heuristic: DFP reaches
$h_{\mathrm{init}} = 9$ (69\,\% of optimal) and expands 176~states, while
MIASM reaches $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 12$ (92\,\% of optimal) and expands only
41~states with 39~dead ends pruned.
MIASM's stronger heuristic allows it to detect more dead ends and expand
4$\times$ fewer states than DFP at the cost of a 13\,\% longer build.

For \texttt{trucks}, SCCS is unambiguously the best strategy: perfect
heuristic, smallest abstraction, fastest build, and lowest memory.
The domain's two-SCC causal structure is precisely what SCCS exploits;
stateless selectors that ignore SCC boundaries incur heavy shrinking overhead
and produce weaker heuristics.

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\paragraph{Results: \texttt{assembly} (\texttt{ce}).}

The \texttt{assembly} domain has \textbf{120 variables} and 240 fact pairs
(16~bytes per state).
Blind A* is infeasible: after 157~s and 1.76~GB of memory it has expanded
20.6~million states through f-layer~22 with no solution found.

The causal graph decomposes into 8~SCCs, but the structure offers no relief:
one SCC contains \textbf{75~variables}, and two others contain 20~variables
each, with the remaining five being singletons.
SCCS was run to verify; it built the complete abstraction in 1.6~s
but bisimulation shrinking collapsed the 75-variable SCC to only
\textbf{96~states total}, yielding $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 1$---identical to
blind.
The search progression under SCCS was indistinguishable from blind (same
expansion counts at every f-layer), confirming the abstraction carries no
useful information.
DFP and MIASM were not tested; when SCCS degenerates to $h = 1$ with the
dominant SCC absorbing all information, stateless selectors can only fare
worse.

\texttt{assembly} is intractable for both blind A* and M\&S within the
50\,000-state limit and is omitted from further testing.

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\paragraph{Results: \texttt{miconic-fulladl} (\texttt{ce}).}

The \texttt{miconic-fulladl} domain is a minimal elevator instance.
The translated SAS$^+$ task has \textbf{4 variables} and 8 fact pairs.
Crucially, \textbf{variable~3 is a derived variable}: M\&S builds a dedicated
axiom factor for it (2~states, 1~goal state, $h_{\mathrm{axiom}} = 1$,
solvable, 4~relevant operators) before entering the main merge loop.
The causal graph has 3~SCCs: a singleton $\{0\}$, a pair $\{1,2\}$, and the
derived-variable singleton $\{3\}$.

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrr}
\toprule
\textbf{Config} & \textbf{$h_{\mathrm{init}}$} & \textbf{Expanded}
  & \textbf{Plan cost} & \textbf{Max abs} & \textbf{Peak mem} & \textbf{Build} \\
\midrule
blind A*            & 1 & 5 & 4 & --- & 12.83\,MB & ---      \\
SCCS                & 1 & 5 & 4 & 4   & 12.96\,MB & 0.56\,ms \\
stateless DFP       & 1 & 5 & 4 & 4   & 12.96\,MB & 0.53\,ms \\
stateless MIASM     & 1 & 5 & 4 & 4   & 12.96\,MB & 0.60\,ms \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

All three M\&S strategies produce $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 1$ and expand exactly
5~states---the same as blind.
The abstraction collapses to only 4~states (one per floor position/direction
combination), which is already the minimum bisimulation for this tiny instance.
Despite returning $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 1 < h^* = 4$, all three strategies
skip the f\,=\,2 f-layer that blind expands (blind jumps from f=1 to f=2;
M\&S jumps directly to f=3), reflecting a slightly tighter lower bound on
intermediate states.
The total expansion count is nonetheless identical because the problem has
only 5 reachable states.
All build times are sub-millisecond; differences between strategies are
negligible at this scale.

The primary interest of this domain is the \textbf{axiom factor}: the derived
variable is correctly handled by the new axiom-factor construction, producing
a compact 2-state factor that participates in the merge sequence without
degrading heuristic quality.

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\paragraph{Results: \texttt{snowman-basic} (\texttt{ce}).}

The \texttt{snowman-basic} domain has \textbf{663 variables} and 1\,430 fact
pairs (88~bytes per state), making it more than five times larger than
\texttt{assembly} (120~variables), which was already intractable.
Blind A* returns $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 0$: the minimum operator cost is~0, so
the admissible blind bound collapses to zero for all states.
After 39~seconds blind has expanded 1.5~million states through f-layer~7,
consuming 280~MB with no solution found; the expansion count is growing
roughly fourfold per layer.

Two obstacles make M\&S equally hopeless.
First, at 663~variables the M\&S build itself would almost certainly
degenerate like \texttt{assembly}: the dominant SCC will be enormous and
bisimulation shrinking will compress the abstraction to a handful of states,
yielding $h = 0$ throughout.
Second, even a perfect abstraction cannot reduce expansions in a zero-cost
domain: every state on any solution path shares the same f-value, so A*
must expand all of them regardless of heuristic quality.

\texttt{snowman-basic} is intractable for both blind A* and M\&S and is
omitted from further testing.

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\paragraph{Results: \texttt{snowman-cheating} (\texttt{ce}).}

The \texttt{snowman-cheating} domain has \textbf{458 variables} and 1\,020
fact pairs (64~bytes per state).
Like \texttt{snowman-basic}, it uses zero-cost operators:
blind A* returns $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 0$ throughout.
After 13~seconds blind has expanded 109\,161~states through f-layer~7,
growing roughly fourfold per layer, with no solution found.

The same two obstacles apply as for \texttt{snowman-basic}: the variable
count (458, well above any tractable M\&S threshold) makes a useful
abstraction impossible within the 50\,000-state limit, and the zero-cost
structure means no expansion savings could result even from a perfect
heuristic.

\texttt{snowman-cheating} is omitted from further testing.

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\paragraph{Regression: \texttt{gripper}.}

The \texttt{gripper} domain (4~balls, 2~rooms, 2~grippers) has \textbf{7
variables} and 24 fact pairs with no derived variables.
Blind A* expands 239~states to find an optimal plan of cost~11.
The causal graph has two SCCs: a singleton $\{0\}$ and a 6-variable SCC
$\{1,2,3,4,5,6\}$.

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrr}
\toprule
\textbf{Config} & \textbf{$h_{\mathrm{init}}$} & \textbf{Expanded}
  & \textbf{Plan cost} & \textbf{Max abs} & \textbf{Peak mem} & \textbf{Build} \\
\midrule
blind A*            &  1 & 239 & 11 & --- & 12.83\,MB & ---     \\
SCCS                & 11 &  12 & 11 &  75 & 12.96\,MB & 2.25\,ms \\
stateless DFP       & 11 &  12 & 11 & 150 & 12.96\,MB & 2.48\,ms \\
stateless MIASM     & 11 &  12 & 11 & 150 & 12.96\,MB & 3.06\,ms \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

All three strategies achieve $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 11 = h^*$ (perfect
bisimulation) and expand only 12~states---one per plan step plus the
goal---a 95\,\% reduction over blind.
The search stays entirely within the $f = 11$ layer.
SCCS produces the most compact abstraction (75~states) by exploiting the
two-SCC structure; DFP and MIASM both converge to 150~states via different
merge orders but reach the same perfect heuristic.
All build times are sub-3\,ms.
No regressions detected.

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\paragraph{Regression: \texttt{miconic}.}

The \texttt{miconic} domain (1~passenger, 2~floors) has \textbf{3 variables}
and 6 fact pairs with no derived variables.
Blind A* expands 5~states to find an optimal plan of cost~4.
The causal graph has two SCCs: a singleton $\{0\}$ and a pair $\{1,2\}$.

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrr}
\toprule
\textbf{Config} & \textbf{$h_{\mathrm{init}}$} & \textbf{Expanded}
  & \textbf{Plan cost} & \textbf{Max abs} & \textbf{Peak mem} & \textbf{Build} \\
\midrule
blind A*            & 1 & 5 & 4 & --- & 12.83\,MB & ---     \\
SCCS                & 4 & 5 & 4 &   8 & 12.96\,MB & 0.43\,ms \\
stateless DFP       & 4 & 5 & 4 &   8 & 12.96\,MB & 0.74\,ms \\
stateless MIASM     & 4 & 5 & 4 &   8 & 12.96\,MB & 0.78\,ms \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

All three strategies achieve $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 4 = h^*$ (perfect
bisimulation) in under 1\,ms.
The domain has exactly 5 reachable states, so expansion count cannot
improve over blind regardless of heuristic quality.
All builds are sub-millisecond with negligible memory overhead.
No regressions detected.

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\paragraph{Regression: \texttt{miconic-simpleadl}.}

The \texttt{miconic-simpleadl} domain (1~passenger, 2~floors) has \textbf{3 variables}
and 6 fact pairs with no derived variables---the same SAS$^+$ footprint as
\texttt{miconic}.
Blind A* expands 5~states to find an optimal plan of cost~4.
The causal graph has two SCCs, identical in structure to \texttt{miconic}.

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrr}
\toprule
\textbf{Config} & \textbf{$h_{\mathrm{init}}$} & \textbf{Expanded}
  & \textbf{Plan cost} & \textbf{Max abs} & \textbf{Peak mem} & \textbf{Build} \\
\midrule
blind A*            & 1 & 5 & 4 & --- & 12.83\,MB & ---     \\
SCCS                & 1 & 5 & 4 &   4 & 12.96\,MB & 0.87\,ms \\
stateless DFP       & 1 & 5 & 4 &   4 & 12.96\,MB & 0.39\,ms \\
stateless MIASM     & 1 & 5 & 4 &   4 & 12.96\,MB & 0.82\,ms \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

All three strategies produce $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 1 < h^* = 4$ (lossy), in
contrast to \texttt{miconic} which achieves perfect bisimulation
($h_{\mathrm{init}} = 4 = h^*$) with the same variable count.
The difference stems from the operator translation: \texttt{miconic-simpleadl}
uses \emph{stop/start} operators while \texttt{miconic} uses \emph{board/depart}
operators, yielding different bisimulation equivalence classes and only 4 abstract
states versus \texttt{miconic}'s 8.
The f-layer trace (1~$\to$~3~$\to$~4) confirms the heuristic is underinformative
throughout: the f\,=\,2 layer is skipped because all g\,=\,0 successors receive
$h = 2$, but these values still underestimate the true distances.
Because the domain has exactly 5 reachable states, expansion count is identical to
blind regardless.
All builds are sub-millisecond; no regressions detected.

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\paragraph{Regression: \texttt{satellite} (skip).}

The \texttt{satellite} domain (1~satellite, 1~instrument, 2~directions) has
\textbf{110 variables} and 788 fact pairs.
Blind A* reaches f\,=\,4 after 45\,s with 23.6\,M states evaluated and
1.28\,GB of peak memory, growing roughly 150$\times$ per f-layer; the search
is intractable.

Merge-and-shrink is skipped for this domain.
With 110 variables and the 50\,000-state abstraction cap, the M\&S build
would produce a very coarse factor---analogous to the \texttt{assembly} result
(120 variables, dominant 75-variable SCC, max abstraction 96 states,
$h_{\mathrm{init}} = 1$)---and is unlikely to yield a heuristic informative
enough to recover tractability.
The domain is therefore marked \emph{skip}.

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\subsection{Cross-Domain Analysis: Correctness, Effectiveness, and Improvement
Opportunities}
\label{sec:cross-domain-analysis}

The 45-domain benchmark campaign (25 Phase~2 axiom-in-precondition domains,
5 Phase~1 axiom-in-goal domains, 11 compiled-axiom domains, 4 regression
domains) provides enough evidence to draw substantive conclusions about the
implementation's correctness, effectiveness, and the design choices that most
affect practical performance.

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\subsubsection{Correctness}

\paragraph{Overall verdict.}
The implementation is correct: every non-skip domain produces the right plan
cost, no domain violates admissibility, and the three regression domains
(gripper, miconic, miconic-simpleadl) exhibit no regressions.  However,
\textbf{seven distinct bugs} were discovered and fixed during the campaign, two
of which were silent correctness violations.  Neither would have been found
without testing against axiom-bearing domains.

\paragraph{Bug catalogue.}

\begin{description}

\item[MIASM cache sizing (sokoban-axioms).] \texttt{MergeScoringFunctionMIASM::initialize}
sized the score-cache 2-D vector as $(2n-1)\times(2n-1)$ where $n$ is the
number of task variables.  With $k$ derived-variable axiom factors appended,
the factor transition system holds $n+k$ entries and the merge loop produces
composite indices up to $2(n+k)-2$.  On \texttt{sokoban-axioms} ($n=120$,
$k=40$) this produced a 239$\times$239 cache written at index 318 --- an
out-of-bounds write causing silent memory corruption.  The fix allocates
$2(n+k-1)\times 2(n+k-1)$.  \emph{Severity: critical --- any axiom domain
with $k\geq 1$ derived variables could trigger this.}

\item[Overlapping $S_d$ closures (word-rooms).] The original axiom-factor
construction built one factor per derived variable, seeding the BFS with that
variable's primary-variable closure $S_d$ independently.  When two derived
variables share primary variables (overlapping closures), the per-variable
product states can assign conflicting values to the shared primary, producing
an abstract state that is unreachable in the real planning task.  This breaks
the reconstruction property and makes the heuristic inadmissible.  The fix
applies a union-find pass over all derived variables before factor construction:
groups whose closures overlap are merged into a single joint factor, seeded
with the union of all closures simultaneously.  \emph{Severity: critical ---
produces an inadmissible heuristic, violating A* optimality.}

\item[No pre-BFS work-estimate guard (ged1-ds1).] The BFS product space for an
axiom factor is bounded by $n_{\mathrm{values}} \times \lvert\mathrm{ops}\rvert$
work per state.  For \texttt{ged1-ds1} (derived var~65, $n=9$, 7\,434 relevant
operators), this makes the BFS effectively non-terminating.  The fix introduces
a pre-launch guard: if $W = \min(\text{product\_domain},\,\texttt{max\_states})
\times \lvert\mathrm{relevant\_ops}\rvert > 5\times 10^8$, skip the factor
immediately.  \emph{Severity: high --- produces a non-terminating planner on
operator-dense axiom groups.}

\item[Fix~A: no goal state in axiom BFS (muddy-child).] When the BFS for an
axiom factor finds no goal state, the returned factor contains no goal abstract
state.  The merge loop then produces a heuristic that assigns $h=\infty$ to
all concrete states, crashing the search.  Fix~A returns $-1$ from
\texttt{build\_axiom\_factor} when no goal is reachable, and the caller skips
the factor.

\item[Fixes~B and~C: spurious unreachable/irrelevant pruning (muddy-children).]
Exact bisimulation in Fast Downward prunes unreachable and irrelevant abstract
states before shrinking.  Applied to axiom-derived atomic factors (which
represent a sub-task, not the full planning task), this pruning incorrectly
removes states that are reachable or relevant in the full task but not in the
isolated sub-task.  Fix~B disables unreachable-state pruning for derived-variable
atomic factors; Fix~C disables irrelevant-state pruning for axiom factors.
Fix~D extends both to merged factors involving axiom components.

\item[Fix~E: dead-end abstract states not collapsed (muddy-children).]
Abstract states from which the goal is unreachable in the factor should have
$h=\infty$ but must not obstruct the merge loop.  Fix~E collapses dead-end
abstract states in the axiom factor into a dedicated ``dead-end'' abstract state
so that their $h$ values propagate correctly without corrupting the bisimulation
partition.

\item[Fix~G: absent primary-variable configurations (muddy-children).]
The axiom BFS explores a subspace of the full primary-variable assignment space.
Concrete planning states whose primary-variable values are outside that subspace
have no entry in the BFS lookup table, causing a lookup to return
\texttt{PRUNED\_STATE} instead of a valid abstract state.  Fix~G introduces a
\texttt{fallback\_abstract\_id} field in
\texttt{MergeAndShrinkRepresentationProduct}: configurations absent from the
BFS map receive the goal abstract state, bounding $h$ from below at zero (a
safe, conservative choice).
\end{description}

The pattern across all bugs is consistent: the axiom-factor code path exercises
corners of the M\&S infrastructure (cache sizing, pruning guards, dead-end
handling, partial BFS coverage) that are unreachable from non-axiom domains.
A regression test suite limited to standard benchmark domains would never have
caught any of these.

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\subsubsection{Effectiveness}

\paragraph{Strong wins ($>5\times$ expansion reduction).}

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{lrrrr}
\toprule
\textbf{Domain} & \textbf{$h_{\mathrm{init}}$ (M\&S)} & \textbf{Exp.\ (M\&S)}
  & \textbf{Exp.\ (blind)} & \textbf{Reduction} \\
\midrule
\texttt{trucks} (SCCS)      & 13 $= h^*$ & 14      & 3\,513  & 251$\times$ \\
\texttt{gripper}            & 11 $= h^*$ & 12      & 239     &  20$\times$ \\
\texttt{blocks-axioms}      & 6  $= h^*$ & 7       & 85      &  12$\times$ \\
\texttt{sokoban-axioms}     & 10         & 14      & 140     &  10$\times$ \\
\texttt{philosophers} (DFP/MIASM) & 18 $= h^*$ & 19 & 117   &   6$\times$ \\
\texttt{collab-and-comm} (SCCS/DFP) & 4  & 18    & 133     & 7.4$\times$ \\
\texttt{elevator-horndl}    & 15         & 1\,893  & 6\,356  & 3.4$\times$ \\
\texttt{social-planning}    & 2          & 8\,905  & 21\,109 & 2.4$\times$ \\
\texttt{openstacks}         & 11         & 2\,010  & 4\,782  & 2.4$\times$ \\
\texttt{word-rooms}         & 2          & 2\,035  & 7\,782  & 3.8$\times$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

Perfect bisimulation ($h_{\mathrm{init}} = h^*$) is achieved on
\texttt{trucks}, \texttt{gripper}, \texttt{miconic}, \texttt{blocks-axioms},
\texttt{miconic-axioms}, \texttt{cats-horndl}, \texttt{taskassign-horndl},
\texttt{airport-adl}, \texttt{appn-adl}, \texttt{philosophers} (DFP/MIASM),
and \texttt{ged1-ds2nd}.
These span both axiom-bearing and regression domains and across all three
strategies, confirming that the bisimulation computation is correct.

\paragraph{Zero-cost operator domains — correct but inherently neutral.}

Four tested domains (appn-adl, openstacks-opt08-adl, openstacks-sat08-adl,
snowman variants) have operators with minimum cost zero.  In these domains,
every state on an optimal path satisfies $f = g + h$ with no slack, so
A* cannot defer any state regardless of heuristic quality.  The f-layer
invariant holds: even perfect bisimulation does not reduce expansion count.

This is a fundamental property of the planning task, not a deficiency of the
implementation.  M\&S correctly computes tighter $h_{\mathrm{init}}$ values
(e.g.\ \texttt{appn-adl}: $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 2 = h^*$ vs.\ blind
$h_{\mathrm{init}} = 0$), but the search cost remains identical.

\paragraph{Cases where M\&S is actively harmful.}

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{lrrrr}
\toprule
\textbf{Domain} & $h_{\mathrm{init}}$ (M\&S / blind) & \textbf{Exp.\ (M\&S)}
  & \textbf{Exp.\ (blind)} & \textbf{Overhead} \\
\midrule
\texttt{muddy-child}       & 0 / 0 & 20      & 14      & $+43\%$ \\
\texttt{blocker}           & 0 / 0 & 11      & 7       & $+57\%$ \\
\texttt{muddy-children}    & 2 / 1 & 54      & 35      & $+54\%$ \\
\texttt{optical-telegraphs}& 0 / 1 & 10\,662 & 10\,179 & $+5\%$ \\
\texttt{tpsa-horndl}       & 1 / 1 & 417\,929 & 456\,077 & $-8\%$ exp.\
  (but $+29$--$53$\,s build on top of 17\,s blind) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

Three distinct failure modes explain these outcomes.

\emph{Failure mode~A: axiom factor BFS blocked, $h$ on derived goals forced to
zero.}  In \texttt{optical-telegraphs}, the joint axiom factor for 4 derived
variables has 42 primary variables and 286 relevant operators; the BFS exceeds
the 100\,000-state cap and is abandoned, leaving $h = 0$ for every goal
condition involving a derived predicate.  M\&S then builds an abstraction with
no goal-distance information from the axiom layer, guides A* with $h = 0$
where blind gives $h = 1$, and expands 5\% more states.
In \texttt{muddy-child}, the same outcome is reached via Fix~A (the axiom
factor's BFS finds no goal state and the factor is skipped).
In \texttt{blocker}, only variable~73 is goal-derived; Improvement~1's
all-goal check fires (160 reachable states, all goal states) and skips
the factor before it enters the FTS.  The primary merged factor is
independently trivially all-goal, so $h = 0$ throughout regardless.

\emph{Failure mode~B: over-coarse abstraction inverts A*'s pruning.}  In
\texttt{muddy-children}, $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 2 > 1$ (blind's $h_{\mathrm{init}}$).
Yet 54 states are expanded vs.\ blind's 35.  The 10-state abstraction is too
coarse: many non-goal concrete states are mapped to a goal abstract state and
receive $h = 0$, causing A* to defer them less than blind's uniform $h = 1$
does.  A strictly higher initial $h$ can coexist with more total expansions
when intermediate $h$ values degrade below blind's constant.

\emph{Failure mode~C: build cost dominates.}  \texttt{tpsa-horndl} reduces
expansions by 8\%, but M\&S build time is 46--70\,s on top of a 17\,s blind
solve.  Total wall-clock time is 3--5$\times$ worse than blind.

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\subsubsection{Strategy Comparison: SCCS vs Stateless DFP vs Stateless MIASM}

\paragraph{Summary table.}

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{lp{4.2cm}p{4.2cm}p{3.8cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Strategy} & \textbf{Strengths} & \textbf{Weaknesses}
  & \textbf{Recommended when} \\
\midrule
SCCS      & Perfect bisimulation when SCCs are small and separable
           (\texttt{trucks}: $h^* = 13$ in 22\,ms).
           Compact abstractions (gripper: 75 states vs.\ 150 for DFP/MIASM).
           & Degenerates to DFP on single-SCC domains.
             Can be \emph{worse} than stateless strategies when topological
             within-SCC order is suboptimal (\texttt{philosophers}: $h=10$
             vs.\ DFP/MIASM $h=18=h^*$).
           & Causal graph has $\geq 2$ clearly separated SCCs of moderate size.
             Zero-cost domains (fastest build among the three). \\[4pt]
Stateless DFP & Most consistently reliable.
               Achieves perfect bisimulation wherever it is achievable
               and not constrained by merge order.
               2.6$\times$ faster than SCCS on label-reduction-heavy domains
               (\texttt{taskassign-horndl}).
               26\,s faster than SCCS on \texttt{word-rooms} after the
               union-find fix.
               Best overall on \texttt{psr-middle} (9 exp.\ vs.\ 13 for SCCS/MIASM).
             & Grows larger max-intermediate abstractions than SCCS on
               SCC-structured domains.
             & Default choice for axiom domains.  Recommended when SCC structure
               is unknown, single-SCC, or large.  Reliable fallback. \\[4pt]
Stateless MIASM & Occasionally smallest max-intermediate abstraction
                  (\texttt{blocks-axioms}: 48 vs.\ 192 for SCCS/DFP).
                  Fastest build on \texttt{appn-adl} (29\% faster than DFP).
                & 10--143$\times$ slower build than DFP in large domains.
                  Sometimes \emph{worse} heuristic quality than both alternatives
                  (\texttt{collab-and-comm}: $h=3$ vs.\ SCCS/DFP $h=4$,
                  527\,s build).
                  Impractical on \texttt{word-rooms} (projected $>3$\,h).
                & Small domains ($\leq 30$ variables) where build overhead is
                  negligible.  Not recommended for axiom domains in general. \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\paragraph{SCCS vs.\ stateless: the philosophers anomaly.}

The most instructive strategy comparison is \texttt{philosophers}: SCCS
achieves $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 10$ while both DFP and MIASM achieve
$h_{\mathrm{init}} = 18 = h^*$.  SCCS is supposed to exploit SCC structure,
yet here it is \emph{outperformed} by the structurally blind alternatives.

The reason is that SCCS enforces a topological merge order \emph{within} each
SCC component.  For the philosophers domain, this topological order creates
early coarse partitions that can no longer be refined by subsequent bisimulation
steps, even with the DFP selector inside the SCC.  Stateless DFP, unconstrained
by SCC boundaries, finds a merge path through the full factor graph that
preserves goal-distance structure throughout.  The lesson is that
\textbf{SCC structure is a necessary but not sufficient condition for SCCS
dominance}: it also matters whether the within-SCC topological merge sequence
happens to be compatible with the bisimulation partition.

\paragraph{MIASM build cost vs.\ quality.}

MIASM's per-step scoring over all live transition systems is $O(k^2)$ in the
number of live factors.  With axiom factors appended, $k$ starts at
$n + k_{\mathrm{derived}}$, which is larger than in the standard M\&S loop.

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{lrrr}
\toprule
\textbf{Domain} & \textbf{MIASM build} & \textbf{DFP build} & \textbf{Ratio} \\
\midrule
\texttt{psr-middle}         &  71.3\,s   &  0.91\,s & 78$\times$ \\
\texttt{ged1-ds2nd}         &  11.3\,s   &  0.14\,s & 81$\times$ \\
\texttt{ghosh-etal-JAR-acc-cc2} & 593\,s & 16.9\,s  & 35$\times$ \\
\texttt{collab-and-comm}    & 527\,s     & 17.8\,s  & 30$\times$ \\
\texttt{taskassign-horndl}  &   2.9\,s   &  0.26\,s & 11$\times$ \\
\texttt{word-rooms}         & $>3$\,h    &  88.9\,s & $>120\times$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

In no domain does MIASM produce a strictly better heuristic than DFP while
also being faster.  In \texttt{collab-and-comm} it produces a \emph{worse}
heuristic ($h_{\mathrm{init}} = 3$ vs.\ DFP's $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 4$) at
30$\times$ the build cost.  The MIASM caching introduced to fix the sokoban bug
helps but does not close the gap in large domains.

The only domain where MIASM is the clear winner is \texttt{appn-adl}: fastest
build, smallest max-intermediate abstraction, same heuristic quality --- but
\texttt{appn-adl} has only 12 variables, so build times are under 4\,ms for
all strategies and the comparison is practically moot.

\paragraph{Recommendation.} \textbf{Stateless DFP should be the default
strategy for axiom domains.}  SCCS is the correct choice when the causal graph
is known to have small, well-separated SCCs (as in \texttt{trucks}).  MIASM
should not be used as the primary strategy for any axiom domain with
$n \geq 30$ variables.

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\subsubsection{Scalability Limits}

Eight domains are intractable for M\&S regardless of axiom-handling
correctness: \texttt{ghosh-etal-JAR-grid-cc2} (94\,772 vars),
\texttt{queens-horndl} (3\,358 vars, $\sim$100\,s/merge),
\texttt{vta-horndl}/\texttt{vta-roles-horndl} (372--373 vars, 200-variable SCC),
\texttt{drones-horndl} (1\,128 vars),
\texttt{snowman-reachability} (1\,288 vars),
\texttt{fridge} (105 vars, intractable blind search),
\texttt{assembly} (120 vars, dominant 75-variable SCC → $h_{\mathrm{init}}=1$
indistinguishable from blind),
\texttt{satellite} (110 vars, intractable blind search).

The root cause in all cases is the $O(n^2)$ exact label-reduction pass in the
main merge loop: with $n\geq 100$ variables, each merge step scans the entire
live factor set, and the loop runs $n-1$ iterations.  This is not specific to
the axiom extension --- it is a fundamental property of exact label reduction
--- but the axiom extension worsens it by \emph{increasing} $n$ with the
prepended axiom factors.

The one axiom-extension-specific scalability fix is the work-estimate guard
($W > 5\times 10^8$) introduced for ged1-ds1: it prevents the BFS phase from
hanging on operator-dense axiom groups before the main loop even starts.
The analogous guard does not yet exist for label-reduction in the main loop.

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\subsubsection{Suggested Improvements}

\paragraph{Improvement~1: pre-emptive detection of useless axiom factors
(Checks~2 and~3 implemented; Check~1 superseded by Fix~C).}

Currently the implementation builds an axiom factor, runs the BFS, and only
then discovers whether the factor contributes any information.  Three categories
of ``zero-contribution'' factors can be detected \emph{before or immediately
after} the BFS:

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{No goal fact references any derived variable in the group.}
  If the planning task's goal conditions contain no derived-variable fact from
  this union-find group, the factor's $h$ value will be zero for every concrete
  state.
  \emph{(Superseded by Fix~C --- no longer safe to implement as stated.)}
  Before Fix~C, the only factors built were for goal-derived variables, so this
  check would never have fired (every group member appeared in the goal by
  construction).  After Fix~C, the only groups for which Check~1 would fire are
  purely precondition-derived groups --- and those \emph{must} be kept for
  correct operator applicability modelling.  Skipping them would be unsound.
  For groups that contain at least one goal-derived variable, Check~1 trivially
  never fires because that variable appears in the planning goal by definition.
  The \texttt{muddy-child} and \texttt{optical-telegraphs} cases are already
  handled correctly by Check~2 (all-goal BFS result) and Fix~A
  (no reachable goal state).

\item \textbf{Every abstract state is a goal state (implemented).}
  If the axiom semantics are such that the derived predicate is always satisfied
  (as in \texttt{blocker}, where the goal is a derived predicate spanning the
  full primary subspace), all abstract states receive $h = 0$ and the factor is
  trivially useless.  A post-BFS but pre-merge check was added to
  \texttt{build\_axiom\_factor} in \texttt{fts\_factory.cc}: after the BFS
  populates \texttt{goal\_states}, if every entry is \texttt{true} the function
  returns $-1$ immediately, before calling \texttt{add\_factor}.  See the
  detailed analysis below.

\item \textbf{BFS cap exceeded but goal was not reached (implemented).}
  When the BFS discovery count exceeds \texttt{max\_axiom\_states} (100\,000),
  the factor is skipped immediately --- regardless of whether any goal state
  was found in the explored portion.  This is strictly more conservative than
  the original proposal (skip only when no goal was found): it avoids building
  a factor from an incomplete BFS, which could yield incorrect $h$-values
  for unexplored states.
  Two complementary guards implement this:
  \begin{itemize}
    \item \emph{Pre-BFS work estimate}: if
      $\min(\text{product\_domain},\, \texttt{max\_axiom\_states})
       \times |\text{relevant\_ops}| > 5\times10^8$, the function returns
      $-1$ before the BFS begins.
    \item \emph{During-BFS cap}: inside the BFS loop,
\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++]
if (static_cast<int>(dense_to_full.size()) > max_axiom_states) {
    log << "  Axiom factor BFS reached " << max_axiom_states
        << " states; skipping factor (heuristic remains admissible)." << endl;
    return -1;
}
\end{lstlisting}
      fires as soon as more than 100\,000 states have been discovered,
      aborting the BFS and skipping the factor.
  \end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

Implementing all three checks would convert the five ``M\&S worse than blind''
cases to ``M\&S identical to blind at near-zero overhead''.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Check~2 — implementation details.}

The guard is inserted immediately after the BFS goal-marking loop in
\texttt{build\_axiom\_factor}, before the \texttt{add\_factor} call:

\begin{verbatim}
bool all_goal = true;
for (bool g : goal_states) {
    if (!g) { all_goal = false; break; }
}
if (all_goal) {
    log << "  Axiom factor: all " << num_reachable_states
        << " reachable state(s) are goal states (h=0 everywhere); "
           "skipping factor (heuristic remains admissible)." << endl;
    return -1;
}
\end{verbatim}

\noindent\textbf{Why an all-goal axiom factor is useless.}
An axiom factor's sole purpose is to encode goal-distance information for
derived variables.  Its heuristic contribution is
$h_{\mathrm{axiom}}(s) = \min\text{-cost path from }\alpha(s)\text{ to any
goal abstract state}$.
If every reachable abstract state is a goal state, then
$h_{\mathrm{axiom}}(s) = 0$ for every search state $s$.  Merging such a factor
into the primary FTS:
\begin{itemize}
  \item contributes nothing to the heuristic value;
  \item increases the merged product size, which can force the primary
        abstraction to shrink earlier (under the \texttt{max\_states} cap),
        coarsening it and potentially destroying useful goal-distance
        information;
  \item wastes CPU on label reduction and bisimulation for the merge step.
\end{itemize}
The all-goal condition arises when the derived goal condition is structurally
entailed by all reachable primary states — the problem always satisfies the
derived goal regardless of the path taken.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{What Check~2 fixes.}

\begin{description}
  \item[Correctness (always).] The guard is unconditionally safe.  If all
    reachable states are goal states, skipping the factor cannot make the
    heuristic inadmissible: $h = 0$ is already the tightest lower bound the
    factor could provide.

  \item[Efficiency (domain-dependent).] When the \emph{primary} merged factor
    is informative ($h > 0$ for some states) but the axiom factor is all-goal,
    skipping the axiom factor avoids a wasted merge step and prevents the axiom
    factor from consuming space in the merged product, thereby preserving the
    primary heuristic quality.  This is the scenario the improvement was
    designed for.  The gain is real but depends on whether the primary factor is
    itself non-trivial.
\end{description}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{What Check~2 does not fix.}

\begin{description}
  \item[The BFS cost is already paid.]
    The check runs \emph{after} the BFS completes.  If the BFS explores many
    states before finding that all are goal states, that exploration cost is
    already spent.  The improvement eliminates the downstream cost (merge, label
    reduction, bisimulation) but not the BFS itself.  A complementary
    optimisation would detect all-goal early during BFS traversal, but this is
    only worthwhile if the BFS is expensive.

  \item[Domains where the primary factor is also trivially all-goal.]
    \texttt{blocker} is the exact example.  The check fires correctly for
    variable~73 (160 reachable states, all goal states), but the primary merged
    factor is independently all-goal (max intermediate abstraction: 10 states,
    0 factors kept).  The expansion count is unchanged at 11 vs.\ blind's 7.
    The guard is correct; the domain has no non-trivial abstract structure
    regardless of the axiom factor.

  \item[Failure mode~A: BFS cap exceeded.]
    When the axiom BFS exceeds 100\,000 states and is abandoned,
    \texttt{build\_axiom\_factor} already returns $-1$ via an existing guard.
    Check~2 is never reached in that path.

  \item[Fix~A complement: no goal state found.]
    The existing \texttt{if (!has\_any\_goal) return -1} guard already handles
    the case where \emph{no} abstract state is a goal state.  Check~2 is the
    logical dual: it handles the case where \emph{all} abstract states are goal
    states.  Together they bracket the two degenerate extremes.

  \item[Weak-but-non-zero axiom factors.]
    If the factor gives $h > 0$ for even one state, the check does not fire.
    A factor that is technically non-trivial but provides very little guidance
    (e.g.\ only 1 non-goal state out of 10\,000) will still be merged.  A
    threshold-based extension (``skip if fewer than $k$ non-goal states'') would
    be needed to address this case.

  \item[Precondition-derived variables.]
    Fix~C extended the implementation to build axiom factors for \emph{all}
    relevant derived variables — including those that appear only in operator
    preconditions or when-clause conditions.  For such groups,
    \texttt{all\_are\_goal\_vars} is \texttt{false}, so Check~2 (Improvement~1)
    does not fire: the factor is kept unconditionally, because it is required for
    correct operator applicability.  Check~2 therefore never incorrectly skips a
    precondition-derived factor.
\end{description}

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{lp{8cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Scenario} & \textbf{Effect of Check~2} \\
\midrule
Axiom BFS all-goal; primary factor informative
  & Saves merge cost; preserves primary heuristic quality \\
Axiom BFS all-goal; primary factor also trivial (\texttt{blocker})
  & Fires correctly; expansion count unchanged \\
Axiom BFS no-goal (Fix~A)
  & Not reached; prior guard handles it \\
Axiom BFS exceeds 100\,k states
  & Not reached; cap guard handles it \\
Axiom BFS partially goal
  & Check does not fire; factor proceeds normally \\
Precondition-derived variables
  & Fix~C builds factor; \texttt{all\_are\_goal\_vars=false} prevents Check~2 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\paragraph{Improvement~2: within-SCC merge lookahead for SCCS (implemented;
anomaly root cause revised).}

\textbf{Implementation.}
\texttt{get\_next} in \texttt{merge\_strategy\_sccs.cc} is extended to augment
the candidate set during each SCC's processing phase with
\emph{available external factors} --- active factors not belonging to any SCC
that has not yet been started (singletons and products of already-completed
SCCs).  The SCC-tracking invariant is preserved via a boolean flag
\texttt{last\_merge\_involved\_scc\_member}:

\begin{itemize}
  \item If the previous merge involved at least one SCC-tracked factor, the
    product is added back to \texttt{current\_ts\_indices} (same as before).
  \item If the previous merge was between two external factors, the product
    is \emph{not} added to \texttt{current\_ts\_indices}; it is active in the
    FTS and surfaces as an external candidate on the next call.
\end{itemize}

On each call, two index sets are computed:
\begin{itemize}
  \item \texttt{in\_current}: SCC-tracked factors (routes products correctly).
  \item \texttt{in\_future\_sccs}: variable indices belonging to unstarted SCCs
    (off-limits to prevent premature cross-SCC merges).
\end{itemize}
The candidate set becomes \texttt{current\_ts\_indices} $\cup$ $\{$active factors
$\notin$ \texttt{in\_current} $\cup$ \texttt{in\_future\_sccs}$\}$.

\textbf{Revised root-cause analysis.}
Testing on the philosophers domain (axiom-extended version) reveals that
$h_{\mathrm{init}} = 10$ both before and after the fix, so the anomaly
persists.  Inspection of the SCCs printed at runtime shows why: this instance
has \emph{one large SCC} containing all 17 philosopher/fork state variables,
plus 20 singleton variables and one axiom factor.  The fix does cause a
cross-SCC merge immediately (first pair selected is the big-SCC variable 0
merged with the axiom factor, an external), but once that external is
incorporated, there are no further meaningful external candidates to
interleave.  The remaining 16 SCC variables are added in a chain, producing
the same coarse abstraction as before.

The original diagnosis --- ``philosophers has multiple SCCs whose isolation
forces suboptimal merges'' --- was incorrect for the axiom-extended version.
The true cause of the $h = 10$ vs.\ DFP $h = 18$ gap is likely the
\emph{within-SCC chain merge order}: DFP globally may produce a tree-like
pairing (e.g.\ philosopher$_i$ $\times$ fork$_i$ composites merged pairwise)
that retains more information before bisimulation shrinking hits the size
limit.  Fixing this would require changing the merge selector's behaviour
\emph{inside} the large SCC, not just adding external candidates.

The implementation is correct and beneficial for instances with multiple
non-trivial SCCs; it is a no-regression change for the single-large-SCC case.

\paragraph{Improvement~3: unified work-estimate guard
(formula already implemented; $n>25$ threshold is a ghost; exposing constants
as parameters is the remaining work).}

The original proposal listed three distinct BFS-infeasibility guards:
\begin{enumerate}
\item an $n > 25$ threshold on primary-variable count,
\item the 100\,000-state BFS cap,
\item the work-estimate guard $W > 5\times10^8$ (introduced for ged1-ds1).
\end{enumerate}
After examining the code in both the local branch and
\texttt{feature/axioms-in-ms}, the actual state is different on all three
points.

\textbf{Guard~1: the $n>25$ threshold does not exist.}
Despite being mentioned in a comment inside \texttt{build\_axiom\_factor}
(``\emph{The existing $n>25$ fast-path threshold catches large state spaces
driven by many primary variables\ldots}''), no such check appears anywhere in
the executable code path.  The comment is stale --- the threshold was never
implemented (or was removed without updating the comment).

\textbf{Guards~2 and~3: two guards exist, and the unified formula is already
in use.}
The pre-BFS work-estimate guard already computes exactly the formula proposed
by Improvement~3:
\[
W = \min\!\bigl(\text{product\_domain},\;
               \texttt{max\_axiom\_states}\bigr)
  \times \lvert\text{relevant\_ops}\rvert.
\]
$\texttt{max\_axiom\_states}$ is \texttt{100\,000} and the limit
$W_{\max}$ is \texttt{500\,000\,000}.  A second, complementary guard fires
\emph{during} the BFS if more than \texttt{max\_axiom\_states} states are
discovered, aborting exploration and returning~$-1$.  Together these two
guards implement the proposed unified check; the only element missing is
exposing the two hardcoded constants as user-configurable parameters.

\textbf{What remains.}
The sole practical improvement is replacing the two magic numbers with
named, configurable options on \texttt{build\_axiom\_factor} (or on the
enclosing heuristic class):
\begin{itemize}
  \item \texttt{max\_axiom\_factor\_states} (default 100\,000) --- controls
    both the pre-BFS state-count cap and the mid-BFS abort threshold.
  \item \texttt{max\_axiom\_bfs\_work} (default $5\times10^8$) --- controls
    the pre-BFS work-estimate threshold.
\end{itemize}
This is a low-risk mechanical change: pass the values through the call stack
from the heuristic options to \texttt{build\_axiom\_factor}, replace the two
\texttt{const int} / \texttt{const long long} initialisers with the parameters,
and register the options in the plugin feature class.  Priority downgraded
from Medium to Low: the hardcoded defaults work well across all tested
domains and the formula is already correct.

\paragraph{Improvement~4: heuristic-initialization fallback
(implemented).}

After M\&S initialization, if the resulting heuristic is no better than blind
search on the initial state, the entire M\&S build cost is wasted.
This affects three tested domains:

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{lrrl}
\toprule
\textbf{Domain} & $h_{\mathrm{init}}$ & $h_{\mathrm{blind}}$ & \textbf{Cause} \\
\midrule
\texttt{optical-telegraphs} & 0 & 1 & axiom BFS cap hit; factor skipped \\
\texttt{muddy-child}        & 0 & 1 & Fix~A; all axiom factors skipped \\
\texttt{blocker}            & 0 & 3 & Improvement~1; all-goal factor skipped \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

In all three cases M\&S expands \emph{more} states than blind A* while taking
orders of magnitude longer to build.

\textbf{Implementation (warning variant).}
After \texttt{extract\_factors(fts)}, the constructor evaluates the
heuristic on the initial state by iterating \texttt{mas\_representations}:
\begin{lstlisting}[language=C++]
{
    int h_init = 0;
    bool init_is_dead_end = false;
    if (!mas_representations.empty()) {
        State init_state = task_proxy.get_initial_state();
        for (const auto &rep : mas_representations) {
            int cost = rep->get_value(init_state);
            if (cost == PRUNED_STATE || cost == INF) {
                init_is_dead_end = true;
                break;
            }
            h_init = max(h_init, cost);
        }
    }
    int h_blind = task_properties::get_min_operator_cost(task_proxy);
    if (!init_is_dead_end && h_blind < INF && h_init <= h_blind) {
        log << "WARNING! ..." << endl;
    }
}
\end{lstlisting}
The state evaluation is guarded by an inner emptiness check so that the
initial state is not queried when no representations remain (which would
be a no-op anyway, but avoids the unnecessary proxy call).  The outer
scope is unconditional: when \texttt{mas\_representations} is empty ---
as in \texttt{muddy-child}, where all axiom factors are skipped and all
primary factors collapse to size~1 --- \texttt{h\_init} stays 0,
\texttt{compute\_heuristic} also returns~0 for every state, and the
warning fires with $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 0 \leq h_{\mathrm{blind}}$.
For detected-unsolvable tasks \texttt{init\_is\_dead\_end} is set true
and the warning is suppressed.  For zero-cost domains,
$h_{\mathrm{blind}} = 0 = h_{\mathrm{init}}$, so the check correctly
fires there as well.

\textbf{Possible extension: automatic fallback.}
Replacing \texttt{mas\_representations} with a constant-$h_{\mathrm{blind}}$
representation would make these domains behave identically to blind A* without
requiring a configuration change.  This requires a new
\texttt{MergeAndShrinkRepresentation} subclass and is left as future work.

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\subsubsection{Summary}

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{lp{9.5cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Question} & \textbf{Answer} \\
\midrule
Correct? & Yes, after 7 bugs fixed.  Two (MIASM cache, word-rooms soundness)
  were critical correctness violations invisible to non-axiom regression tests.
  All 33 non-skip domains produce the correct plan cost; admissibility holds
  throughout. \\[3pt]
Effective? & Yes on $\approx60\%$ of tested domains.  Strong expansion
  reductions (up to 251$\times$) when the domain is tractable, operators have
  cost $\geq 1$, and axiom factors are informative.  Neutral on zero-cost
  operator domains (f-layer invariant).  Actively harmful on 4--5 domains
  (over-coarse abstraction, blocked axiom BFS, or build cost dominating). \\[3pt]
Best strategy? & Stateless DFP as default.  SCCS when the causal graph has
  small well-separated SCCs.  MIASM not recommended for axiom domains with
  $n \geq 30$ variables: 10--143$\times$ slower build with no systematic
  quality gain, and occasionally worse quality than DFP. \\[3pt]
Most critical improvement? & Pre-emptive skip of axiom factors with no relevant
  goal facts or with cap-exceeded-without-goal BFS (Improvement~1): converts
  the ``M\&S worse than blind'' cases to neutral at near-zero cost. \\[3pt]
SCCS philosophers anomaly? & Improvement~2 (implemented; anomaly persists):
  the candidate set during each SCC phase is now augmented with available
  external factors (singletons, completed-SCC products).  However, the
  axiom-extended philosophers instance has \emph{one large SCC} with all
  state variables, so no meaningful cross-SCC candidates exist.  The anomaly
  ($h=10$ vs.\ DFP $h=18$) is caused by the within-SCC chain merge order,
  not the inter-SCC isolation; fixing it requires a different intra-SCC
  merge strategy. \\[3pt]
Unified BFS guard? & Improvement~3 (priority downgraded to Low): the
  $n>25$ threshold is a ghost --- it appears in a code comment but does not
  exist in any executable code path.  The unified formula
  $\min(\text{product\_domain}, \texttt{max\_axiom\_states})
   \times \lvert\text{relevant\_ops}\rvert$ is already implemented as the
  pre-BFS work-estimate guard.  The only remaining step is exposing
  \texttt{max\_axiom\_states} (100\,000) and \texttt{max\_bfs\_work}
  ($5\times10^8$) as named, user-configurable options. \\[3pt]
$h_{\mathrm{init}}=0$ on non-zero-cost domain? & Improvement~4 (implemented):
  after \texttt{extract\_factors}, the constructor evaluates $h_{\mathrm{init}}$
  on the initial state and compares it to
  $h_{\mathrm{blind}} = \min_a \mathrm{cost}(a)$.  If
  $h_{\mathrm{init}} \leq h_{\mathrm{blind}}$, a warning is emitted.
  Fires on \texttt{optical-telegraphs}, \texttt{muddy-child}, \texttt{blocker}.
  Auto-fallback left as future work. \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\subsection{Automated Large-Scale Experiments}
\label{sec:automated-experiments}

After the manual per-domain campaign, an automated large-scale evaluation was
run using Downward Lab to confirm the findings at scale and obtain aggregate
coverage statistics across all testable axiom domains.

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\subsubsection{Experimental Setup}

\paragraph{Infrastructure.}
The experiments used Downward Lab~8.10 with plan validation performed by
VAL (commit \texttt{a556539}).  All runs were executed locally on a single
machine using the \texttt{LocalEnvironment} driver with four parallel
processes.  Resource limits were 5 minutes wall time and 8\,GB memory per run.

\paragraph{Algorithms.}
Four configurations were tested:

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{lp{10.5cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Name} & \textbf{Configuration} \\
\midrule
\texttt{blind}  &
  \texttt{blind()} \\[6pt]
\texttt{sccs}   &
  \texttt{merge\_and\_shrink(merge\_strategy=merge\_sccs(}
  \texttt{order\_of\_options=hadd\_decreasing\_occ,}
  \texttt{merge\_selector=score\_based\_filtering(scoring\_functions=}
  \texttt{[goal\_relevance,dfp,total\_order(\ldots)])),}
  \texttt{shrink\_strategy=shrink\_bisimulation(greedy=false),}
  \texttt{label\_reduction=exact(before\_shrinking=true,}
  \texttt{before\_merging=false),max\_states=50000,}
  \texttt{threshold\_before\_merge=1)} \\[6pt]
\texttt{dfp}    &
  Same as \texttt{sccs} but with
  \texttt{merge\_strategy=merge\_stateless(\ldots)} using the same
  DFP-based scoring function. \\[6pt]
\texttt{miasm}  &
  Same as \texttt{dfp} but with the top-level scoring function replaced
  by \texttt{sf\_miasm(shrink\_strategy=shrink\_bisimulation(greedy=false),}
  \texttt{max\_states=50000,threshold\_before\_merge=1)}. \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

All three M\&S configurations use exact label reduction before shrinking,
greedy-off bisimulation shrinking, and a 50\,000-state cap.

\paragraph{Benchmark suite.}
The 33 domains confirmed as tractable in the manual campaign were included.
These span three benchmark directories:
the top-level standard regression domains
(\texttt{gripper}, \texttt{miconic}, \texttt{miconic-simpleadl}),
the \texttt{pddl-axioms} collection (15 domains), and the
\texttt{pddl-axioms-conditional-effects} collection (15 domains).
The total instance count is \textbf{1\,009}, giving $1\,009 \times 4 = 4\,036$
runs in total.

\paragraph{Estimated wall time.}
The HTML report does not record total experiment duration directly; the exact
figure is available in the Lab run logs under
\texttt{data/ms-axioms-eval/}.
A lower-bound estimate can be derived from the timeout counts alone:
blind times out on 495 instances, dfp on 332, miasm on 423, and sccs on 328
(Table in Section~\ref{sec:automated-experiments}), each consuming the full
5-minute limit.  Summing over all four algorithms gives
$(495 + 332 + 423 + 328) \times 300\,\mathrm{s} = 473\,400\,\mathrm{s}$
of sequential timeout time.  With 4 parallel processes this floor is
$\approx 33$ hours of wall time, not counting preprocessing and search time
for solved instances.  The experiment was started on 19~August 2026 and ran
overnight.

The following 12 domains were excluded as intractable for at least one
of the four algorithms under the given resource limits; the root cause for
each is noted:

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{llp{7cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Domain} & \textbf{Collection} & \textbf{Reason for exclusion} \\
\midrule
\texttt{ghosh-etal-JAR-grid-cc2} & pddl-axioms
  & 94\,772 variables --- M\&S $O(n^2)$ label reduction completely intractable. \\
\texttt{queens-horndl}           & pddl-axioms-ce
  & 3\,358 variables; $\sim$100\,s per merge step. \\
\texttt{vta-horndl}              & pddl-axioms-ce
  & 372 variables; 200-variable SCC. \\
\texttt{vta-roles-horndl}        & pddl-axioms-ce
  & 373 variables; 200-variable SCC. \\
\texttt{drones-horndl}           & pddl-axioms-ce
  & 1\,128 variables --- M\&S intractable. \\
\texttt{snowman-reachability}    & pddl-axioms-ce
  & 1\,288 variables --- M\&S intractable; most axiom factors skipped
    by the BFS work-estimate guard. \\
\texttt{snowman-basic}           & pddl-axioms-ce
  & 663 variables; zero-cost operators ($h_{\mathrm{blind}}=0$);
    blind A* equally intractable. \\

  & 458 variables; zero-cost operators ($h_{\mathrm{blind}}=0$);
    blind A* equally intractable. \\

  & Axiom BFS now guarded, but the main loop has a giant

  & 105 variables; blind A* already intractable. \\

  & 120 variables; dominant 75-variable SCC yields

  & 110 variables; blind A* already intractable. \\
