\paragraph{Metrics.}
A custom Lab parser extracted \texttt{search\_start\_time},
\texttt{search\_start\_memory}, and \texttt{initial\_h\_value}
(from the pattern \texttt{f = X [1 evaluated, 0 expanded, \ldots]} in
\texttt{run.log}) in addition to the standard Lab attributes
(\texttt{coverage}, \texttt{cost}, \texttt{expansions}, \texttt{search\_time},
\texttt{total\_time}, \texttt{memory}).  All plans were validated by VAL.

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\subsubsection{Results}

\paragraph{Overall coverage.}

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{lrr}
\toprule
\textbf{Algorithm} & \textbf{Coverage (/ 1009)} & \textbf{\%} \\
\midrule
blind  & 506 & 50.1 \\
sccs   & 487 & 48.3 \\
dfp    & 484 & 47.9 \\
miasm  & 396 & 39.2 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

Blind achieves the highest raw coverage because it has zero preprocessing
overhead and never times out during heuristic construction.  SCCS and DFP
are nearly equivalent (3 instances apart).  MIASM solves 22\% fewer instances
than blind and 18\% fewer than DFP, confirming the manual finding that its
$O(k^2)$ per-step scoring overhead is prohibitive within 5 minutes.

\paragraph{Plan costs (optimality check).}
All four algorithms produce identical optimal plan costs on every instance
they solve, confirming that the M\&S implementation preserves admissibility.
The total-cost sums differ only because different subsets of instances are
solved (blind: 5\,507; M\&S strategies: 5\,531).

\paragraph{Heuristic quality.}

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{lr}
\toprule
\textbf{Algorithm} & $\boldsymbol{\sum} h_{\mathrm{init}}$ (finite) \\
\midrule
blind  &     507 \\
dfp    & 7\,643 \\
sccs   & 7\,642 \\
miasm  & 6\,452 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

DFP and SCCS produce essentially identical heuristic quality (7\,643
vs.\ 7\,642 summed over solved instances), both achieving approximately
$15\times$ higher initial $h$-values than blind.  MIASM produces weaker
heuristics (6\,452) despite its higher construction cost.

\paragraph{Error breakdown.}

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{lrrrr}
\toprule
\textbf{Error type} & \textbf{blind} & \textbf{dfp} & \textbf{miasm} & \textbf{sccs} \\
\midrule
Timeout (search out of time)                  & 495 & 332 & 423 & 328 \\
Exit code 248 ($h=\infty$ at initial state)   &   0 & 167 & 168 & 168 \\
Detected unsolvable                           &   8 &  24 &  20 &  24 \\
Out of memory                                 &   0 &   2 &   1 &   2 \\
SIGKILL (OS kill)                             &   0 &   0 &   1 &   0 \\
\midrule
\textbf{Solved (``success'')}                 & 499 & 481 & 394 & 484 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

Exit code~248 arises only for M\&S algorithms and corresponds to instances
where the heuristic assigns $h=\infty$ to the initial state (the abstraction
correctly detects a dead end or the task is proved unsolvable during construction).
Blind times out on 495 instances; M\&S strategies time out far less (328--423),
confirming that the heuristic guidance is effective on the instances where
construction completes.
The single MIASM SIGKILL occurred on a large \texttt{appn-adl} instance.

\textbf{Tied rows} (all algorithms equal, shown in grey in the HTML report):
\texttt{gripper}, \texttt{miconic}, \texttt{miconic-simpleadl},
\texttt{cats-horndl}, \texttt{collab-and-comm}, \texttt{muddy-child},
\texttt{muddy-children}, \texttt{sum}, \texttt{ghosh-etal-JAR-acc-cc2},
\texttt{openstacks}, and \texttt{social-planning} (11 of 33 domains).

\textbf{M\&S dominates blind}:
\texttt{miconic-axioms} (56 vs.\ 151), \texttt{blocks-axioms} (19 vs.\ 29),
\texttt{grid-axioms} (2 vs.\ 4), \texttt{appn-adl} (11 vs.\ 16).
The clearest win is \texttt{miconic-axioms}: DFP and SCCS solve all 151
instances while blind solves only 56.

\textbf{Blind dominates M\&S}:
\texttt{taskassign-horndl} (21 vs.\ 3), \texttt{robot-horndl} (44 vs.\ 15),
\texttt{robotConj-horndl} (44 vs.\ 15), \texttt{miconic-fulladl} (73 vs.\ 53),
\texttt{sokoban-axioms} (23 vs.\ 15), \texttt{psr-middle} (25 vs.\ 19),
\texttt{psr-large} (15 vs.\ 13), \texttt{ged1-ds2nd} (13 vs.\ 7).
These domains share a common trait: M\&S build time dominates the 5-minute
limit before meaningful search can begin.

\textbf{MIASM notably weaker}:
\texttt{miconic-axioms} (79 vs.\ 151 for DFP/SCCS),
\texttt{sokoban-axioms} (12 vs.\ 15 for DFP),
\texttt{elevator-horndl} (19 vs.\ 21 for DFP/SCCS).
Curiously, MIASM is the sole winner in \texttt{trucks} (8 vs.\ 7 for all
others), though the margin is a single instance.

\textbf{DFP vs.\ SCCS}: the two strategies differ on only 6 domains
(\texttt{psr-large}: 9 vs.\ 13; \texttt{psr-middle}: 18 vs.\ 19;
\texttt{airport-adl}: 17 vs.\ 16; \texttt{appn-adl}: 16 vs.\ 15;
\texttt{sokoban-axioms}: 15 vs.\ 14; \texttt{blocker}: 3 vs.\ 4).
SCCS is ahead on 3 and DFP on the other 3; neither dominates.

\textbf{The SCCS philosophers anomaly does not appear at the coverage level.}
Manual testing (Section~\ref{sec:cross-domain-analysis}) established that
SCCS achieves $h_{\mathrm{init}} = 10$ while DFP achieves
$h_{\mathrm{init}} = 18 = h^*$ on the axiom-extended philosophers instance,
a consequence of the within-SCC chain merge order producing coarse partitions
that bisimulation cannot later refine.
Yet the automated results show identical coverage: both strategies solve
exactly 6 of the 49 philosophers instances.
The reason is that the 6 solvable instances are small (2--4 philosophers),
and even with the weaker heuristic SCCS closes the search well within the
5-minute limit.
The anomaly is real but manifests in \emph{search effort}, not in coverage:
SCCS expands more nodes than DFP on philosophers because $h=10$ provides less
guidance than $h=18$.
Coverage is a coarse metric: a strategy can be systematically weaker in
heuristic quality without that weakness crossing the threshold of costing a
solved instance, as long as the remaining benchmark instances are small enough
to be tractable regardless of heuristic accuracy.
The anomaly would become a coverage difference on larger philosophers instances,
where the gap between $h=10$ and $h=18$ would determine whether the search
fits within the time limit.

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\subsubsection{Conclusions}

The automated experiments on 1\,009 instances confirm and quantify the
conclusions drawn from the manual campaign:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{DFP and SCCS are equivalent in practice.}
    They produce virtually identical heuristic quality (7\,643 vs.\ 7\,642
    summed $h_{\mathrm{init}}$ values) and differ by only 3 instances in
    coverage.  Neither consistently outperforms the other across all domains.

  \item \textbf{MIASM is not suitable for axiom domains at scale.}
    Its 22\% lower coverage compared to blind (and 18\% lower than DFP) is
    caused by the $O(k^2)$ per-step scoring overhead.  With axiom factors
    appended, $k$ starts larger and the overhead becomes prohibitive within
    a 5-minute limit.

  \item \textbf{The tradeoff with blind is domain-dependent.}
    M\&S wins on axiom-rich structured domains (\texttt{miconic-axioms}:
    56 vs.\ 151) but loses on domains where preprocessing dominates
    (\texttt{taskassign-horndl}: 21 vs.\ 3).  This motivates future work on a
    configurable \texttt{axiom\_factor\_mode} parameter that would control
    which axiom factors are included, enabling selective inclusion of only
    the factors that improve coverage.

  \item \textbf{Optimality is preserved.}
    All algorithms produce identical plan costs on every shared instance,
    confirming that the axiom-handling implementation is admissible across
    all 33 tested domains at scale.
\end{enumerate}

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\subsection{Implemented: Configurable Axiom-Factor Baselines}
\label{sec:next-steps-baselines}

The automated results show a clear but domain-dependent tradeoff: M\&S
axiom handling is strongly beneficial on some domains
(\texttt{miconic-axioms}: 56 $\to$ 151) and actively harmful on others
(\texttt{taskassign-horndl}: 21 $\to$ 3) compared to blind search.
What remained unknown was \emph{which aspect} of the axiom-factor construction
drives the benefit.  Is it the axiom factors themselves?  Only the goal-derived
ones?  Or does the BFS cap (which discards expensive factors early) matter most?

To answer these questions, an \texttt{axiom\_factor\_mode} parameter has been
implemented that controls how axiom factors are selected before they enter the
M\&S main loop.  The parameter is an integer $0$--$3$ registered with the
Fast Downward plugin system, with default value~$3$ (\texttt{all\_capped}).

\subsubsection{Implemented Modes}

Four modes cover the key points in the design space.

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{cllp{6.5cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Value} & \textbf{Mode} & \textbf{Short name} & \textbf{Description} \\
\midrule
0 & \texttt{none}        & No axiom factors
  & Skip axiom-factor construction entirely.  The M\&S loop runs only over
    atomic transition systems for ordinary planning variables.  Closest
    baseline to standard (non-axiom) M\&S; isolates whether the axiom
    extension contributes at all. \\[4pt]
1 & \texttt{only\_goal}  & Goal-derived, uncapped
  & Build axiom factors only for derived variables that appear in the planning
    goal; no BFS work-estimate guard applied.  Purely precondition-derived
    groups are excluded.  Safe in practice because goal-derived closures are
    small; tests whether goal-relevance alone explains the benefit. \\[4pt]
2 & \texttt{only\_goal\_capped} & Goal-derived, capped
  & Same as \texttt{only\_goal} but additionally applies the BFS
    work-estimate guard ($W > 5\times10^8$): a goal-derived factor whose
    construction would exceed the work limit is skipped.  Isolates whether
    the tractable goal-derived factors alone suffice. \\[4pt]
3 & \texttt{all\_capped} (default) & All factors, capped
  & \textbf{Current default behaviour}: build one factor per union-find group
    of derived variables, including purely precondition-derived groups, subject
    to the BFS work-estimate guard and the mid-BFS state-count cap. \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\paragraph{Why \texttt{all} (uncapped) is excluded.}

A fifth candidate mode --- \emph{all axiom factors with no BFS work-estimate
guard} --- is intentionally omitted.  The BFS work-estimate guard was
introduced precisely because removing it causes non-termination on
operator-dense axiom groups.

For an axiom factor, the BFS product domain grows \emph{exponentially} in
the number of primary variables in the closure.  With $k$ multi-valued primary
variables each taking $d$ values, the product domain is $d^k$, which for
even modest $k$ and operator-dense groups results in an effectively unbounded
search.  The guard fires pre-launch when the estimate
\[
  W = \min(\text{product\_domain},\;100\,000)
      \times \lvert\text{relevant\_ops}\rvert
      > 5\times 10^8
\]
and skips the factor immediately.  For precondition-derived groups, both
$\text{product\_domain}$ and $\lvert\text{relevant\_ops}\rvert$ can be very
large: in \texttt{ged1-ds1}, the axiom group for derived variable~65 has
9 multi-valued primary variables and $K = 7\,434$ relevant operators.
The product domain for those 9 variables runs into the tens of millions, so
at 7\,434 operators per state the BFS work is effectively unbounded.
Removing the guard on \emph{all} groups (including precondition-derived ones
with large closures) would reproduce this failure on any domain with an
operator-dense axiom group, making the planner non-terminating without an
external resource limit.

Goal-derived groups, by contrast, tend to have smaller closures because their
primary-variable set is bounded by the goal structure.  This is why
\texttt{only\_goal} (uncapped) is safe enough to include as a baseline:
in practice the guard very rarely triggers for goal-derived factors, and the
few cases where it would can be caught by the overall planner time limit.

\subsubsection{Diagnostic Value of Each Comparison}
\label{sec:diagnostic-value}

\begin{description}
  \item[\texttt{all\_capped} vs.\ \texttt{none}]
    Isolates the total contribution of the current axiom extension.
    If \texttt{none} matches or exceeds \texttt{all\_capped} on a domain,
    the axiom factors provide no net benefit there under default settings.

  \item[\texttt{only\_goal\_capped} vs.\ \texttt{none}]
    Tests whether restricting to capped goal-derived factors alone is
    sufficient to recover the benefit of \texttt{all\_capped}.

  \item[\texttt{all\_capped} vs.\ \texttt{only\_goal\_capped}]
    Measures the marginal value of precondition-derived axiom factors.
    If the two are equivalent in coverage, precondition-derived factors
    can be dropped without loss, and \texttt{only\_goal\_capped} becomes
    the recommended default.

  \item[\texttt{only\_goal} vs.\ \texttt{only\_goal\_capped}]
    Measures the cost of the BFS work-estimate guard specifically for
    goal-derived factors.  A large gap would indicate that many beneficial
    goal-derived factors are being discarded by the guard, motivating a
    looser cap or smarter selection criterion.
\end{description}

\subsubsection{Implementation Details}

The implementation spans four files.  The central design decision is to
separate \emph{variable selection} from the \emph{BFS work cap}: which derived
variables deserve an axiom factor is decided by \texttt{collect\_relevant\_derived\_variables},
and whether the per-factor BFS is capped is decided by a separate boolean
\texttt{apply\_cap} passed to \texttt{build\_axiom\_factor}.

\paragraph{\texttt{merge\_and\_shrink\_algorithm.h}}

Declares the \texttt{AxiomFactorMode} enum before the class:
\begin{lstlisting}
enum class AxiomFactorMode { NONE, ONLY_GOAL, ONLY_GOAL_CAPPED, ALL_CAPPED };
\end{lstlisting}
Adds an \texttt{axiom\_factor\_mode} field to \texttt{MergeAndShrinkAlgorithm},
extends the constructor signature accordingly, and updates the return type of
\texttt{get\_merge\_and\_shrink\_algorithm\_arguments\_from\_options} to a
10-element tuple whose last element is \texttt{AxiomFactorMode}.

\paragraph{\texttt{merge\_and\_shrink\_algorithm.cc}}

Replaces the earlier fixed variable-collection logic with a static helper:
\begin{lstlisting}
static vector<int> collect_relevant_derived_variables(
    const TaskProxy &task_proxy, AxiomFactorMode mode) {
    if (mode == AxiomFactorMode::NONE)
        return {};
    // Mark goal-derived variables.
    vector<bool> needs_axiom_factor(num_variables, false);
    for (FactProxy goal : task_proxy.get_goals())
        if (goal.get_variable().is_derived())
            needs_axiom_factor[goal.get_variable().get_id()] = true;
    // For ALL_CAPPED, also mark precondition- and effect-condition-derived.
    if (mode == AxiomFactorMode::ALL_CAPPED) {
        for (OperatorProxy op : task_proxy.get_operators()) {
            for (FactProxy pre : op.get_preconditions())
                if (pre.get_variable().is_derived()) ...
            for (EffectProxy eff : op.get_effects())
                for (FactProxy cond : eff.get_conditions())
                    if (cond.get_variable().is_derived()) ...
        }
    }
    // Only include variables that actually have axiom rules.
    ...
    return result;
}
\end{lstlisting}

The rationale for the \texttt{has\_axiom\_rule} filter: some derived variables
are translator-introduced constants that are always set to their initial value
and never changed by any axiom rule.  Their existing atomic factor is already
correct, and \texttt{build\_axiom\_factor} assumes its target has at least one
axiom rule, so calling it on a rule-less variable would produce a degenerate
empty closure.

Just before the \texttt{build\_axiom\_factor} call, the cap flag is computed:
\begin{lstlisting}
bool apply_cap = (axiom_factor_mode == AxiomFactorMode::ONLY_GOAL_CAPPED ||
                  axiom_factor_mode == AxiomFactorMode::ALL_CAPPED);
int axiom_index = build_axiom_factor(
    task_proxy, derived_var_ids, fts, log,
    &pending_var_order, &pending_state_values, apply_cap);
\end{lstlisting}

Two additional soundness steps are performed after each factor is built:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Dead-end merging.}  Any axiom-factor state whose goal distance
    is $\infty$ is merged into the nearest goal state.  Without this, the
    abstract heuristic would return $h = \infty$ for concrete task states that
    map to those abstract dead-ends --- which may be spurious (the concrete
    state might still reach the goal via operators not captured in the factor's
    partial subspace) --- violating admissibility.  Merging such states into the
    goal state gives $h = 0$, which is admissible since $0 \le h^*$.

  \item \textbf{Derived-variable atomic factor collapsing.}  After the axiom
    factor is built, each atomic factor for a derived variable in the group is
    collapsed to a single abstract state.  Operators cannot set derived
    variables (only axioms can), so the atomic transition systems for derived
    variables have no operator transitions.  Without collapsing, irrelevance
    pruning would mark the initial state (value = \texttt{false}) as
    goal-irrelevant and return $h = \infty$ --- again spuriously inadmissible.
    Collapsing all states of such a factor into one prevents this while still
    allowing the main loop to merge the collapsed factor into the axiom factor
    later.
\end{enumerate}

The option is registered in the plugin feature class:
\begin{lstlisting}
feature.add_option<int>("axiom_factor_mode",
    "0=none, 1=only_goal, 2=only_goal_capped, 3=all_capped (default)",
    "3", Bounds("0", "3"));
\end{lstlisting}

\paragraph{\texttt{fts\_factory.h} and \texttt{fts\_factory.cc}}

\texttt{build\_axiom\_factor} receives a new trailing parameter:
\begin{lstlisting}
extern int build_axiom_factor(..., bool apply_work_cap = true);
\end{lstlisting}
Inside, the pre-BFS size guard is wrapped:
\begin{lstlisting}
if (apply_work_cap && n > 0 && !relevant_ops.empty()) {
    // guard: if W = min(product_domain, 100000) * |relevant_ops| > 5e8, skip
}
\end{lstlisting}
When \texttt{apply\_work\_cap = false} (mode \texttt{ONLY\_GOAL}), the guard is
bypassed and the BFS proceeds unconditionally.

\paragraph{\texttt{merge\_and\_shrink\_heuristic.h/.cc}}

These files receive only mechanical parameter threading: the
\texttt{AxiomFactorMode} value flows through the constructor and is forwarded
to \texttt{MergeAndShrinkAlgorithm}.  The \texttt{make\_shared\_from\_arg\_tuples}
call in \texttt{create\_component} unpacks the 10-element algorithm tuple
automatically, requiring no further changes.

\paragraph{Diagnostic logging}

A log line is emitted at the start of \texttt{build\_factored\_transition\_system}
to make the mode's effect immediately visible:
\begin{lstlisting}
axiom_factor_mode=3: selected 2 derived variable(s) for axiom factors var1 var2.
\end{lstlisting}
This is followed by \texttt{build\_axiom\_factor}'s own per-factor log lines:
\begin{lstlisting}
Building axiom factor for derived variable(s) 1 2.
  Axiom factor BFS: 1 primary vars, 2 relevant ops
  all 2 reachable state(s) are goal states (h=0 everywhere); skipping factor.
\end{lstlisting}
The ``skipping factor'' message above illustrates a case --- observed on
\texttt{miconic-axioms} --- where the entire reachable product state space
consists of goal states, making the axiom factor uniformly $h = 0$ and
therefore useless.  The code detects this and discards the factor, which is
correct: a factor contributing only $h = 0$ adds no information to the
heuristic and can be safely omitted.

\subsubsection{Experimental Setup}

The experiment runs five configurations (blind search as reference, plus the
four modes) using stateless DFP (\texttt{merge\_stateless} + DFP scoring
functions) only.  MIASM is excluded: it is too slow at scale and does not add
diagnostic information.  The same 33 domains and 1\,009 instances are used,
with the same resource limits (5 minutes, 8\,GB).
Results are reported in Section~\ref{sec:modes-results}.

% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
% ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
\subsubsection{Experimental Results}
\label{sec:modes-results}

The five-configuration experiment (blind plus modes 0--3) was run on
2026-09-03 using Downward Lab~8.10, 16 parallel processes,
5-minute wall-time limit, and 8\,GB memory per run over the full 33-domain,
1\,009-instance suite.

\paragraph{Coverage.}

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{clr}
\toprule
\textbf{Mode} & \textbf{Config} & \textbf{Coverage} \\
\midrule
 --  & \texttt{blind}                & 485 \\
  0  & \texttt{dfp-none}             & \textbf{486} \\
  1  & \texttt{dfp-only-goal}        & 478 \\
  2  & \texttt{dfp-only-goal-capped} & 478 \\
  3  & \texttt{dfp-all-capped}       & 451 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

Mode~0 (\texttt{none}) marginally outperforms blind ($+1$ instance).
Modes~1 and~2 are equal ($-7$ vs.\ blind).  Mode~3 is the weakest ($-34$):
it produces 163 out-of-memory exits during M\&S construction because building
axiom factors for all derived variables --- including precondition-derived
groups with large primary-variable closures --- frequently exhausts the 8\,GB
limit before search begins.

\paragraph{Admissibility.}

Total plan cost summed over all jointly solved instances:

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{clrr}
\toprule
\textbf{Mode} & \textbf{Config} & \textbf{Total cost} &
  \textbf{$\Delta$ vs.\ blind} \\
\midrule
 --  & \texttt{blind}                & 4852 & --- \\
  0  & \texttt{dfp-none}             & 4852 & $0$ \\
  1  & \texttt{dfp-only-goal}        & 4852 & $0$ \\
  2  & \texttt{dfp-only-goal-capped} & 4852 & $0$ \\
  3  & \texttt{dfp-all-capped}       & 4876 & $\mathbf{+24}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

Modes~0, 1, and~2 never exceed blind's cost on any instance across all
33 domains --- they are admissible in practice.  Mode~3 returns plans of
strictly higher cost than blind on 10 instances of \texttt{miconic-fulladl}
(excess $+1$ to $+4$ steps per instance, total $+24$), violating admissibility.
\texttt{miconic-fulladl} is the only domain in the current suite with the
structural pattern that triggers the bug (derived variables appearing in
operator preconditions or effect conditions but not in the planning goal),
but the defect is general: any domain with that pattern can exhibit it.

\paragraph{Findings per diagnostic comparison.}

The comparisons defined in Section~\ref{sec:diagnostic-value} are answered
as follows.

\begin{description}

  \item[\texttt{all\_capped} vs.\ \texttt{none}]
    Mode~3 (451) falls 35 instances \emph{below} mode~0 (486).  The axiom
    extension as currently implemented in \texttt{all\_capped} is a net
    \emph{negative}: the memory overhead of building precondition-derived
    axiom factors eliminates more runs than the heuristic guidance recovers.

  \item[\texttt{only\_goal\_capped} vs.\ \texttt{none}]
    Mode~2 (478) falls 8 instances below mode~0 (486).  Restricting to
    goal-derived factors reduces the memory cost substantially, but does not
    fully close the gap to mode~0.  Goal-derived axiom factors improve
    heuristic quality on some domains, but the M\&S build overhead still
    causes more timeouts than the improved guidance prevents.

  \item[\texttt{only\_goal} vs.\ \texttt{only\_goal\_capped}]
    Coverage is identical (478 each), confirming that the BFS work-estimate
    guard rarely fires for goal-derived variable groups: their primary-variable
    closures are small enough that the guard threshold ($W > 5 \times 10^8$)
    is almost never exceeded.  The cap adds no cost and can be retained
    as a safety net without affecting results.

  \item[\texttt{all\_capped} vs.\ \texttt{only\_goal\_capped}]
    Mode~3 (451) is 27 instances worse than mode~2 (478) and is additionally
    inadmissible.  Precondition-derived axiom factors provide no coverage
    benefit --- they consume memory, trigger out-of-memory exits, and
    introduce an admissibility bug.  There is currently no case for including
    them as a default.

\end{description}

\paragraph{Root cause of mode~3 inadmissibility.}

When \texttt{build\_axiom\_factor} is called for a non-goal derived variable~$V$,
the BFS treats ``$V$ has value $v_{\text{pre}}$'' --- the value required by some
operator precondition --- as the goal of the factor.  States from which
$v_{\text{pre}}$ is unreachable are identified as dead ends and, by the
dead-end merging step, folded into the goal-absorbing state.  When this axiom
factor is subsequently merged with the rest of the M\&S abstraction, the
product abstraction requires \emph{both} the actual planning goal \emph{and}
$V = v_{\text{pre}}$ to hold for a merged abstract state to be a goal state.
Concrete states in which $V \ne v_{\text{pre}}$ but the actual planning goal
\emph{is} satisfied therefore receive $h = \infty$ in the merged abstraction.
Since these states may be reachable with finite true cost (the actual goal does
not constrain~$V$), the heuristic overestimates and A* can return a suboptimal
plan.

\paragraph{Fix: correct goal projection for non-goal factors.}

The root cause is that \texttt{build\_axiom\_factor} uses $V = v_{\text{pre}}$ as the
BFS target for non-goal derived variables.  The fix is to replace this artificial goal
with a projection of the actual planning goal onto the primary variables in $V$'s
closure:

\begin{itemize}

  \item If the actual planning goal constrains at least one primary variable in $V$'s
    closure, use those goal assignments as the BFS target.  The resulting factor is
    admissible by construction: goal states in the factor correspond exactly to
    concrete states where the planning goal, restricted to those primary variables,
    is satisfied.

  \item If the actual planning goal constrains none of the primary variables in $V$'s
    closure, every BFS state is a goal state, the factor is uniformly $h = 0$, and
    it is automatically discarded.

\end{itemize}

The change is localised to \texttt{build\_axiom\_factor} and
\texttt{collect\_relevant\_derived\_variables}.  Pending this fix,
\textbf{mode~2 (\texttt{only\_goal\_capped})} is the recommended safe default:
admissible, equal coverage to mode~1, and protected against non-termination on
operator-dense axiom groups by the BFS work-estimate guard.  Mode~3 remains
available as an experimental option but must not be used as the default until
the admissibility bug is resolved.

\subsubsection{Action Plan}
\label{sec:action-plan}

The following steps are ordered by priority.

\begin{enumerate}

  \item \textbf{Fix mode~3 admissibility (correctness, high priority).}
    Change the goal definition used by \texttt{build\_axiom\_factor} for
    non-goal derived variables: instead of using $V = v_{\text{pre}}$ as
    the BFS target, project the actual planning goal onto the primary
    variables in $V$'s closure.  If the actual goal does not constrain any
    of those primary variables, every BFS state becomes a goal state and
    the factor is automatically discarded.  The fix is localised to
    \texttt{build\_axiom\_factor} and
    \texttt{collect\_relevant\_derived\_variables}, and is verifiable in a
    single run on \texttt{miconic-fulladl}.

  \item \textbf{Change the default from mode~3 to mode~2 (trivial, high
    priority).}  Independent of the bug fix: mode~2 (\texttt{only\_goal\_capped})
    is already implemented, admissible, and outperforms mode~3 in both
    coverage and correctness.  This is a one-line change to the option
    registration default value in \texttt{merge\_and\_shrink\_algorithm.cc}
    and should be applied immediately.

  \item \textbf{Re-run the experiment with fixed mode~3 (medium priority).}
    After step~1, re-run the five-configuration experiment to determine
    whether the corrected mode~3 recovers any coverage over mode~2, or
    whether it remains equivalent.  This determines whether mode~3 retains
    any scientific justification once the admissibility bug is removed.

  \item \textbf{Investigate why mode~0 marginally outperforms blind (medium
    priority).}  Mode~0 adds no axiom factors yet covers one more instance
    than blind.  This is unexpected: without axiom factors the M\&S
    abstraction differs from blind only through label reduction and
    bisimulation shrinking.  Identifying the domain and instance responsible
    for the gap clarifies whether standard M\&S already provides a useful
    baseline independently of the axiom extension.

  \item \textbf{Investigate why modes~1 and~2 lose 7 instances vs.\ mode~0
    (medium priority).}  Goal-derived axiom factors should improve heuristic
    quality; losing 7 instances relative to no axiom factors suggests that
    the build cost (time or memory) of those factors causes timeouts or
    out-of-memory exits that mode~0 avoids.  Profiling which domains and
    instances are affected will guide whether a tighter work-estimate guard
    or a size cap on goal-derived factors could close the gap.

\end{enumerate}
